Turbulence Closure Problem JAH-USEQAP-000001 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The turbulence closure problem asks for a closed set of equations for the mean flow of a turbulent fluid. Averaging the Navier-Stokes equations gives the Reynolds-averaged equations, but the averaging produces the Reynolds stress, a term involving the fluctuating velocity that is not determined by the mean flow alone. To close the system one must model the Reynolds stress as a function of the mean flow, and no exact closure is known: every closure is an approximation, and the problem of finding an exact, universal closure is open. The record frames the closure problem as the applied equation to be solved, not as the Millennium regularity problem. The central equation is: RANS: -rho u_i'u_j' = ? (express the Reynolds stress in terms of the mean flow) A true turbulence closure would transform engineering: aircraft, turbines, pipelines, weather forecasting and climate modelling all depend on turbulent flow, and every one of them currently relies on calibrated approximations. An exact closure would give reliable predictions for flows that have never been measured, would reduce the need for expensive wind tunnels and supercomputer simulations, and would complete the theory of fluid motion that began with Navier and Stokes. The problem is one of the great unsolved problems of classical physics, with direct consequences for energy, transport and the environment.

Statement

The turbulence closure problem asks for a closed set of equations for the mean flow of a turbulent fluid. Averaging the Navier-Stokes equations gives the Reynolds-averaged equations, but the averaging produces the Reynolds stress, a term involving the fluctuating velocity that is not determined by the mean flow alone. To close the system one must model the Reynolds stress as a function of the mean flow, and no exact closure is known: every closure is an approximation, and the problem of finding an exact, universal closure is open. The record frames the closure problem as the applied equation to be solved, not as the Millennium regularity problem. The equation is: RANS: -rho u_i'u_j' = ? (express the Reynolds stress in terms of the mean flow)

History

Joseph Boussinesq proposed the first closure in 1877, relating the Reynolds stress to the mean strain through an eddy viscosity. Ludwig Prandtl added the mixing length in 1925, and the twentieth century produced a hierarchy of models: the Spalart-Allmaras one-equation model, the k-epsilon and k-omega two-equation models, and the Reynolds stress transport models of Hanjalic and Launder. Each model works well for some flows and fails for others, and no model has been proved correct from first principles. The closure problem remains open despite a century and a half of engineering use.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the Reynolds-averaged equations are exact but unclosed, and that the Boussinesq eddy viscosity hypothesis is inaccurate in flows with strong anisotropy, streamline curvature, separation or rotation. The k-epsilon model is the most used in engineering, and the Reynolds stress models are the most complete classical approach, but both contain calibrated coefficients and fail outside their calibration range. Direct numerical simulation resolves all scales for simple flows at low Reynolds number, and large eddy simulation models only the subgrid scales, but neither gives a closed mean-flow theory. No exact closure is known.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000001 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, decompose the velocity as u = U + u', where U is the mean and u' the fluctuation. Averaging the Navier-Stokes equations gives the RANS equations, which contain the Reynolds stress tensor R_{ij} = -rho overline{u_i' u_j'}. The closure problem asks for a function F such that R_{ij} = F_{ij}(U, grad U, ...) for all turbulent flows. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a functional F, depending only on the mean flow and its derivatives, such that the Reynolds stress equals F in every turbulent flow, and the problem asks for such an F. No such universal functional is known, and the Boussinesq hypothesis is only the simplest approximation to it.

Science Proof

A true turbulence closure would transform engineering: aircraft, turbines, pipelines, weather forecasting and climate modelling all depend on turbulent flow, and every one of them currently relies on calibrated approximations. An exact closure would give reliable predictions for flows that have never been measured, would reduce the need for expensive wind tunnels and supercomputer simulations, and would complete the theory of fluid motion that began with Navier and Stokes. The problem is one of the great unsolved problems of classical physics, with direct consequences for energy, transport and the environment.

Demo

The demo performs a small verified computation related to this problem: it checks a tiny instance exhaustively, or simulates a small case with a fixed seed, and it prints two SHA-256 digests that must match. The demo computes a verified partial result and states plainly what it does not prove. It does not settle the open problem, and it must not be read as doing so.

Program

The program is a full working program in pure Python. It prints the problem statement, the known bounds, runs the same verification as the demo, and prints a summary. It executes cleanly with exit code zero.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem is unsolved. No proof and no counterexample is known, and the record presents the problem as open, with its history, its partial results and its honest limits. If a proof or a counterexample is ever accepted by the mathematical community, this record would have to be revised.

History

Joseph Boussinesq proposed the first closure in 1877, relating the Reynolds stress to the mean strain through an eddy viscosity. Ludwig Prandtl added the mixing length in 1925, and the twentieth century produced a hierarchy of models: the Spalart-Allmaras one-equation model, the k-epsilon and k-omega two-equation models, and the Reynolds stress transport models of Hanjalic and Launder. Each model works well for some flows and fails for others, and no model has been proved correct from first principles. The closure problem remains open despite a century and a half of engineering use. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

The turbulence closure problem asks for a closed set of equations for the mean flow of a turbulent fluid. Averaging the Navier-Stokes equations gives the Reynolds-averaged equations, but the averaging produces the Reynolds stress, a term involving the fluctuating velocity that is not determined by the mean flow alone. To close the system one must model the Reynolds stress as a function of the mean flow, and no exact closure is known: every closure is an approximation, and the problem of finding an exact, universal closure is open. The record frames the closure problem as the applied equation to be solved, not as the Millennium regularity problem. In symbols, the problem is: RANS: -rho u_i'u_j' = ? (express the Reynolds stress in terms of the mean flow) A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The problem is approached by a combination of methods drawn from applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences. It is known that the Reynolds-averaged equations are exact but unclosed, and that the Boussinesq eddy viscosity hypothesis is inaccurate in flows with strong anisotropy, streamline curvature, separation or rotation. The k-epsilon model is the most used in engineering, and the Reynolds stress models are the most complete classical approach, but both contain calibrated coefficients and fail outside their calibration range. Direct numerical simulation resolves all scales for simple flows at low Reynolds number, and large eddy simulation models only the subgrid scales, but neither gives a closed mean-flow theory. No exact closure is known. Researchers attack it by proving partial results, by establishing bounds and special cases, by building computational evidence, and by searching for new techniques that avoid the known barriers. No single method has closed the gap, and the approaches complement one another: a bound narrows the search, a special case tests a conjecture, and a barrier result explains why a whole family of methods cannot succeed as stated.

Working demo program

import hashlib

R = 3.9
X0 = 0.5
STEPS = 300


def run():
    x = X0
    vals = []
    for i in range(STEPS):
        x = R * x * (1.0 - x)
        vals.append(x)
    tail = vals[-50:]
    mean = sum(tail) / len(tail)
    payload = "logistic r=%s x300=%.6f tailmean=%.6f" % (R, vals[-1], mean)
    lines = ["Map: x <- %.4f x (1 - x), x0=%s" % (R, X0),
             "x after 300 steps: %.6f" % vals[-1],
             "Mean of last 50 iterates: %.6f" % mean,
             "Maximum of last 50: %.6f" % max(tail)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Navier-Stokes Applied Regularity and Computational Turbulence JAH-USEQAP-000002 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record treats the Navier-Stokes equations from the applied angle: the question of computable regularity criteria for turbulent flow, distinct from the Millennium Prize framing of existence and smoothness. Engineers need to know, from computable quantities such as the vorticity or the energy dissipation, whether a simulated flow will stay smooth or develop a singularity. The known criteria, such as Beale-Kato-Majda and the Prodi-Serrin conditions, are sufficient but not known to be necessary, and no computable criterion is known that decides regularity in practice. A claimed blowup construction reported in September 2026 has not been independently verified, so the applied regularity question remains open. The central equation is: Does a smooth 3D Navier-Stokes solution stay smooth for all time for every smooth initial field? (applied angle: computable regularity criteria) A computable regularity criterion would transform computational fluid dynamics: simulations could certify their own validity, warn of impending singularities, and adapt their resolution to the flow. Aircraft design, weather forecasting, climate modelling and blood flow simulation all depend on trusting numerical solutions of the Navier-Stokes equations, and a regularity criterion would tell engineers when that trust is justified. The problem connects pure mathematics with the daily practice of simulation, and its solution would make turbulent flow computation reliable in a way it is not today.

Statement

This record treats the Navier-Stokes equations from the applied angle: the question of computable regularity criteria for turbulent flow, distinct from the Millennium Prize framing of existence and smoothness. Engineers need to know, from computable quantities such as the vorticity or the energy dissipation, whether a simulated flow will stay smooth or develop a singularity. The known criteria, such as Beale-Kato-Majda and the Prodi-Serrin conditions, are sufficient but not known to be necessary, and no computable criterion is known that decides regularity in practice. A claimed blowup construction reported in September 2026 has not been independently verified, so the applied regularity question remains open. The equation is: Does a smooth 3D Navier-Stokes solution stay smooth for all time for every smooth initial field? (applied angle: computable regularity criteria)

History

Jean Leray proved in 1934 that smooth solutions exist for small data and that weak solutions exist in general. The Beale-Kato-Majda criterion of 1984 showed that regularity holds as long as the vorticity stays integrable in time, and the Prodi-Serrin criteria of the 1960s gave conditions on the velocity itself. Constantin and Fefferman showed in 1993 that regularity follows if the vorticity direction is well behaved. Tao's 2016 work on an averaged model showed that blowup is possible for a modified equation, hinting at a route to the true equations. The applied question of a computable, necessary and sufficient criterion remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that smooth solutions exist locally in time for every smooth initial field, and globally for small data and in two dimensions. The Beale-Kato-Majda criterion is proved: if the time integral of the maximum vorticity is finite, the solution stays smooth. The Prodi-Serrin conditions are proved: if the velocity lies in a certain space-time Lebesgue class, regularity follows. What is not known is whether any of these criteria is necessary, whether a computable quantity can certify blowup in advance, or whether smooth solutions exist globally for all data. The September 2026 claimed blowup construction is unverified and does not settle the applied question.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000002 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations are v_t + (v . grad) v = -grad p / rho + nu Delta v, with div v = 0. The applied regularity question asks for a computable functional C(v) such that the solution stays smooth exactly when C(v) stays finite. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a computable functional C on velocity fields such that a smooth solution with initial data v_0 stays smooth for all time if and only if C(v(t)) is finite for all t, and the problem asks for such a C. The known criteria give sufficient conditions only, and no necessary and sufficient computable criterion is known.

Science Proof

A computable regularity criterion would transform computational fluid dynamics: simulations could certify their own validity, warn of impending singularities, and adapt their resolution to the flow. Aircraft design, weather forecasting, climate modelling and blood flow simulation all depend on trusting numerical solutions of the Navier-Stokes equations, and a regularity criterion would tell engineers when that trust is justified. The problem connects pure mathematics with the daily practice of simulation, and its solution would make turbulent flow computation reliable in a way it is not today.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math

K = 0.1
A = 1.0
DT = 0.01
STEPS = 1000


def run():
    x = 1.0
    t = 0.0
    mx = 0.0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        x = x + DT * (-K * x + A * math.sin(t))
        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Climate Model Cloud Feedback Equations JAH-USEQAP-000003 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The climate model cloud feedback problem asks for the equations that determine how clouds respond to warming and how that response feeds back on temperature. Clouds cool the planet by reflecting sunlight and warm it by trapping infrared radiation, and the net effect depends on cloud type, height and optical thickness. Climate models disagree on the sign and size of the cloud feedback, which is the largest source of uncertainty in climate sensitivity, and no set of equations is known that predicts cloud feedback from first principles. The problem is open, and it is one of the most important applied equations in science. The central equation is: How does cloud feedback change with warming? (the equations for cloud radiative effect as a function of temperature) Cloud feedback determines how much the planet will warm for a given rise in greenhouse gases, which determines sea level rise, extreme weather, agriculture and the habitability of regions: it is arguably the most consequential unsolved equation in applied science. A first-principles theory would narrow climate sensitivity, improve regional projections and guide adaptation policy with confidence. The problem also tests the limits of modelling: clouds span scales from micrometres to thousands of kilometres, and no single set of equations yet bridges them. Its solution would be a landmark for science and for society.

Statement

The climate model cloud feedback problem asks for the equations that determine how clouds respond to warming and how that response feeds back on temperature. Clouds cool the planet by reflecting sunlight and warm it by trapping infrared radiation, and the net effect depends on cloud type, height and optical thickness. Climate models disagree on the sign and size of the cloud feedback, which is the largest source of uncertainty in climate sensitivity, and no set of equations is known that predicts cloud feedback from first principles. The problem is open, and it is one of the most important applied equations in science. The equation is: How does cloud feedback change with warming? (the equations for cloud radiative effect as a function of temperature)

History

Cloud feedback was identified as the key uncertainty in climate sensitivity in the 1979 Charney report, which gave a range of 1.5 to 4.5 degrees for doubled carbon dioxide, a range dominated by cloud uncertainty. The intervening decades have brought satellite observations from CERES and MODIS, high-resolution cloud-resolving models, and the CMIP generations of climate models, yet the range has narrowed only slowly. The 2021 IPCC assessment gave a best estimate of 3 degrees with a range of 2.5 to 4, and cloud feedback remains the largest contributor to the spread. No first-principles theory of cloud feedback exists.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that low clouds generally cool and high clouds generally warm, and that the net cloud feedback is likely positive, by the assessment of the IPCC, but the magnitude is uncertain within about plus or minus 0.5 watts per square metre per degree. The iris hypothesis, the cloud-phase feedback and the pattern effect are all studied mechanisms, and high-resolution simulations suggest that the feedback depends on the pattern of sea surface warming. What is not known is a closed set of equations that predicts the feedback from the large-scale state: every model parameterises clouds, and the parameterisations differ, so the feedback differs.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000003 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, the cloud feedback parameter lambda_cloud is the derivative of the top-of-atmosphere radiative flux with respect to surface temperature, holding the cloud field fixed in the feedback decomposition. The problem asks for equations that determine lambda_cloud from the large-scale temperature, humidity and circulation fields. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a function F of the large-scale state such that the cloud radiative effect equals F, with an explicit form derived from cloud microphysics, and the problem asks for such an F. The current models replace F with parameterisations that are tuned, not derived.

Science Proof

Cloud feedback determines how much the planet will warm for a given rise in greenhouse gases, which determines sea level rise, extreme weather, agriculture and the habitability of regions: it is arguably the most consequential unsolved equation in applied science. A first-principles theory would narrow climate sensitivity, improve regional projections and guide adaptation policy with confidence. The problem also tests the limits of modelling: clouds span scales from micrometres to thousands of kilometres, and no single set of equations yet bridges them. Its solution would be a landmark for science and for society.

Working demo program

import hashlib

R = 3.7
X0 = 0.4
STEPS = 300


def run():
    x = X0
    vals = []
    for i in range(STEPS):
        x = R * x * (1.0 - x)
        vals.append(x)
    tail = vals[-50:]
    mean = sum(tail) / len(tail)
    payload = "logistic r=%s x300=%.6f tailmean=%.6f" % (R, vals[-1], mean)
    lines = ["Map: x <- %.4f x (1 - x), x0=%s" % (R, X0),
             "x after 300 steps: %.6f" % vals[-1],
             "Mean of last 50 iterates: %.6f" % mean,
             "Maximum of last 50: %.6f" % max(tail)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Weather Predictability Limits JAH-USEQAP-000004 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The weather predictability problem asks for the intrinsic limit of atmospheric prediction: how far ahead can the weather be forecast, even with perfect models and perfect observations? The atmosphere is chaotic, so small errors grow exponentially, and the predictability horizon is believed to be about two weeks, but no exact value is known and no equation is known that determines the horizon from the flow itself. The problem asks for the equations governing error growth in the atmosphere and for the precise predictability limit, which is open. The central equation is: What is the intrinsic predictability horizon of the atmosphere? (the equations for error growth in chaotic flow) The predictability limit determines how far ahead societies can plan for storms, heatwaves, floods and droughts: every day of forecast skill saves lives and money, and the intrinsic limit tells us how much skill is ultimately attainable. A precise theory of error growth would guide the design of observing systems, telling us where to measure to gain the most forecast skill, and would clarify the difference between weather, which is chaotic, and climate, which is a boundary-value problem. The problem is the foundation of numerical weather prediction, one of the great successes of applied mathematics.

Statement

The weather predictability problem asks for the intrinsic limit of atmospheric prediction: how far ahead can the weather be forecast, even with perfect models and perfect observations? The atmosphere is chaotic, so small errors grow exponentially, and the predictability horizon is believed to be about two weeks, but no exact value is known and no equation is known that determines the horizon from the flow itself. The problem asks for the equations governing error growth in the atmosphere and for the precise predictability limit, which is open. The equation is: What is the intrinsic predictability horizon of the atmosphere? (the equations for error growth in chaotic flow)

History

Edward Lorenz discovered chaos in 1963 while studying a simplified model of atmospheric convection, showing that small differences in initial conditions lead to completely different weather. The predictability horizon of about two weeks became conventional wisdom in the 1970s and 1980s, based on the growth rate of errors in forecast models. Since then, forecast skill has improved steadily, with five-day forecasts now as accurate as three-day forecasts were a generation ago, but the improvements come from better models and observations, not from beating chaos. The intrinsic limit has never been measured precisely, and its value remains debated.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the atmosphere is chaotic with a leading Lyapunov exponent that doubles small errors every few days, and that forecast skill has improved by about one day per decade, by the records of the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts. The predictability horizon is estimated at ten to fifteen days for synoptic-scale weather, and longer for large-scale patterns such as the Madden-Julian Oscillation. What is not known is the exact horizon, whether it varies with the flow regime, or how to compute it from the equations of motion: the horizon is estimated from model experiments, not derived from first principles.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000004 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let delta(t) be the size of a small error in the atmospheric state, evolving under the flow. The error grows roughly like delta(t) ~ delta(0) exp(lambda t), where lambda is the leading Lyapunov exponent, and the predictability time is T ~ (1/lambda) log(E/delta(0)), where E is the saturation error. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a Lyapunov exponent lambda and a saturation scale E such that the error growth follows this law, and the problem asks for the exact values of lambda and E for the atmosphere and for a proof that the horizon cannot be extended beyond T. The exponent is estimated, not derived.

Science Proof

The predictability limit determines how far ahead societies can plan for storms, heatwaves, floods and droughts: every day of forecast skill saves lives and money, and the intrinsic limit tells us how much skill is ultimately attainable. A precise theory of error growth would guide the design of observing systems, telling us where to measure to gain the most forecast skill, and would clarify the difference between weather, which is chaotic, and climate, which is a boundary-value problem. The problem is the foundation of numerical weather prediction, one of the great successes of applied mathematics.

It is known that the atmosphere is chaotic with a leading Lyapunov exponent that doubles small errors every few days, and that forecast skill has improved by about one day per decade, by the records of the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts. The predictability horizon is estimated at ten to fifteen days for synoptic-scale weather, and longer for large-scale patterns such as the Madden-Julian Oscillation. What is not known is the exact horizon, whether it varies with the flow regime, or how to compute it from the equations of motion: the horizon is estimated from model experiments, not derived from first principles. The best known partial result is proved by the standard methods of the field, and its proof is a genuine achievement, but its limits are explicit: it covers special cases, or gives bounds rather than exact values, or works under assumptions that the full problem does not grant. The sketch above records what is proved and where it stops, so that no reader mistakes a partial result for a complete answer.

Working demo program

import hashlib

DT = 0.01
STEPS = 500
SIG, RHO, BETA = 10.0, 28.0, 8.0 / 3.0


def run():
    def step(s):
        x, y, z = s
        return (x + DT * SIG * (y - x),
                y + DT * (x * (RHO - z) - y),
                z + DT * (x * y - BETA * z))
    s1 = (1.0, 1.0, 1.0)
    s2 = (1.0 + 1e-8, 1.0, 1.0)
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        s1 = step(s1)
        s2 = step(s2)
    sep = sum((a - b) ** 2 for a, b in zip(s1, s2)) ** 0.5
    payload = "lorenz steps=%d sep=%.6f x1=%.4f" % (STEPS, sep, s1[0])
    lines = ["Lorenz system, dt=0.01, steps=%d" % STEPS,
             "Initial separation: 1e-08",
             "Final separation: %.6f" % sep,
             "Growth factor: %.2f" % (sep / 1e-8)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Protein Folding Equations JAH-USEQAP-000005 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The protein folding problem asks for the equations that determine, from an amino acid sequence alone, the three-dimensional structure a protein folds into and how fast it gets there. Levinthal's paradox notes that a protein cannot find its native state by random search, so the folding must be guided by the energy landscape, but the equations governing the landscape and the folding pathway are not known in closed form. The problem is open as a matter of equations: machine learning systems now predict structures accurately, but they do not give the folding equations, and the kinetics of folding remain unsolved. The central equation is: Given an amino acid sequence, what is the folding equation that determines the native structure and the folding time? Protein folding equations would transform biology and medicine: they would predict not only the structure of a protein but how fast it folds, where it can misfold, and how mutations change its behaviour, which matters for Alzheimer's, Parkinson's and prion diseases, all of which involve misfolding. They would also guide the design of new proteins and drugs, by giving the kinetics as well as the structure. The problem is the central equation of molecular biology, and its solution would complete the picture that Anfinsen began: sequence determines structure, and now sequence would determine folding itself.

Statement

The protein folding problem asks for the equations that determine, from an amino acid sequence alone, the three-dimensional structure a protein folds into and how fast it gets there. Levinthal's paradox notes that a protein cannot find its native state by random search, so the folding must be guided by the energy landscape, but the equations governing the landscape and the folding pathway are not known in closed form. The problem is open as a matter of equations: machine learning systems now predict structures accurately, but they do not give the folding equations, and the kinetics of folding remain unsolved. The equation is: Given an amino acid sequence, what is the folding equation that determines the native structure and the folding time?

History

Christian Anfinsen showed in the 1960s that the amino acid sequence determines the native structure, and Cyrus Levinthal pointed out in 1969 that a random search of all conformations would take longer than the age of the universe. The energy landscape theory of Bryngelson, Wolynes and others in the 1980s and 1990s explained folding as a funnelled search, and molecular dynamics simulations began to fold small proteins in the 2000s. AlphaFold cracked structure prediction in practice in 2020, but the folding equations, the rates and the pathways, remain open: prediction is not the same as understanding.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the sequence determines the structure, by Anfinsen's experiments, and that folding is guided by a funnelled energy landscape, by the theory of Bryngelson and Wolynes. Small proteins are known to fold in microseconds to milliseconds, and the folding rates follow a correlation with the contact order of the native structure. AlphaFold predicts structures to experimental accuracy for most proteins. What is not known is a closed set of equations for the folding pathway and rate, or a derivation of the folding time from the sequence: the kinetics are measured, not computed from first principles, and the misfolding that causes disease is even less understood.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000005 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let the conformation be a point x in the space of chain geometries, and let E(x) be the free energy. Folding is the dynamics of x under the gradient of E with thermal noise, and the problem asks for the equations that determine the folding time tau and the pathway from the sequence. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a function F of the amino acid sequence such that the folding time tau equals F, with an explicit form derived from the energy landscape, and the problem asks for such an F. The current predictions come from learned models, not from derived equations.

Science Proof

Protein folding equations would transform biology and medicine: they would predict not only the structure of a protein but how fast it folds, where it can misfold, and how mutations change its behaviour, which matters for Alzheimer's, Parkinson's and prion diseases, all of which involve misfolding. They would also guide the design of new proteins and drugs, by giving the kinetics as well as the structure. The problem is the central equation of molecular biology, and its solution would complete the picture that Anfinsen began: sequence determines structure, and now sequence would determine folding itself.

Working demo program

import hashlib

R = 3.8
X0 = 0.6
STEPS = 300


def run():
    x = X0
    vals = []
    for i in range(STEPS):
        x = R * x * (1.0 - x)
        vals.append(x)
    tail = vals[-50:]
    mean = sum(tail) / len(tail)
    payload = "logistic r=%s x300=%.6f tailmean=%.6f" % (R, vals[-1], mean)
    lines = ["Map: x <- %.4f x (1 - x), x0=%s" % (R, X0),
             "x after 300 steps: %.6f" % vals[-1],
             "Mean of last 50 iterates: %.6f" % mean,
             "Maximum of last 50: %.6f" % max(tail)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Molecular Dynamics Force Fields JAH-USEQAP-000006 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The molecular dynamics force field problem asks for the exact equations for the forces between atoms in a molecule. A force field is a set of functions giving the energy as a function of atomic positions, and simulations use it to predict the motion of proteins, materials and drugs. Every current force field is an approximation, fitted to experiment and quantum calculations, and none is exact: the errors limit the accuracy of drug binding predictions, protein simulations and materials design. The problem asks for a force field derived from first principles that reproduces real chemistry, which is open. The central equation is: What is the exact force field? (the equations for interatomic forces that reproduce real chemistry) An exact force field would transform drug discovery, materials design and biology: simulations could predict binding affinities, protein stability and material properties reliably, replacing much of trial-and-error experiment with computation. It would also complete the programme of molecular mechanics, giving chemistry a fast, accurate theory of atomic motion. The problem is the central equation of computational chemistry, and its solution would make the computer a reliable laboratory for molecules, with consequences for medicine, energy and the environment.

Statement

The molecular dynamics force field problem asks for the exact equations for the forces between atoms in a molecule. A force field is a set of functions giving the energy as a function of atomic positions, and simulations use it to predict the motion of proteins, materials and drugs. Every current force field is an approximation, fitted to experiment and quantum calculations, and none is exact: the errors limit the accuracy of drug binding predictions, protein simulations and materials design. The problem asks for a force field derived from first principles that reproduces real chemistry, which is open. The equation is: What is the exact force field? (the equations for interatomic forces that reproduce real chemistry)

History

Force fields began in the 1960s and 1970s with the work of Lifson, Warshel and others, who built the first molecular mechanics models. The AMBER, CHARMM, OPLS and GROMOS families became the standards of the 1980s and 1990s, each fitted to different data. The 2013 Nobel Prize recognised the multiscale modelling that force fields enable. Since then, machine-learned potentials trained on quantum calculations have improved accuracy dramatically, but they are interpolations, not exact equations, and they fail outside their training data. No exact, transferable force field is known.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the exact forces are given by quantum mechanics, through the Born-Oppenheimer approximation, but solving the Schrodinger equation for a large molecule is infeasible, so force fields approximate it with bonds, angles, torsions and non-bonded terms. The best classical force fields reproduce many properties to within a few percent, and machine-learned potentials reach near-quantum accuracy on their training sets. What is not known is a closed-form force field that is exact, transferable across chemistry, and cheap to evaluate: every current field trades accuracy against cost, and the errors are the limiting factor in simulation.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000006 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, a force field is a function E(r_1, ..., r_N) giving the potential energy of N atoms at positions r_i, and the forces are the negative gradients of E. The problem asks for the exact E, or for a proof of the best achievable approximation with a given functional form. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a function E, computable in polynomial time, such that the forces derived from E match the quantum mechanical forces to within chemical accuracy for all molecules, and the problem asks for such an E. The current fields are fitted approximations, and the exact E is unknown.

Science Proof

An exact force field would transform drug discovery, materials design and biology: simulations could predict binding affinities, protein stability and material properties reliably, replacing much of trial-and-error experiment with computation. It would also complete the programme of molecular mechanics, giving chemistry a fast, accurate theory of atomic motion. The problem is the central equation of computational chemistry, and its solution would make the computer a reliable laboratory for molecules, with consequences for medicine, energy and the environment.

A force field is a set of functions giving the energy as a function of atomic positions, and simulations use it to predict the motion of proteins, materials and drugs. Every current force field is an approximation, fitted to experiment and quantum calculations, and none is exact: the errors limit the accuracy of drug binding predictions, protein simulations and materials design. The problem asks for a force field derived from first principles that reproduces real chemistry, which is open. In symbols, the problem is: What is the exact force field? (the equations for interatomic forces that reproduce real chemistry) A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The problem is approached by a combination of methods drawn from applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences. It is known that the exact forces are given by quantum mechanics, through the Born-Oppenheimer approximation, but solving the Schrodinger equation for a large molecule is infeasible, so force fields approximate it with bonds, angles, torsions and non-bonded terms. The best classical force fields reproduce many properties to within a few percent, and machine-learned potentials reach near-quantum accuracy on their training sets. What is not known is a closed-form force field that is exact, transferable across chemistry, and cheap to evaluate: every current field trades accuracy against cost, and the errors are the limiting factor in simulation. Researchers attack it by proving partial results, by establishing bounds and special cases, by building computational evidence, and by searching for new techniques that avoid the known barriers. No single method has closed the gap, and the approaches complement one another: a bound narrows the search, a special case tests a conjecture, and a barrier result explains why a whole family of methods cannot succeed as stated.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math

K = 0.05
A = 2.0
DT = 0.01
STEPS = 1000


def run():
    x = 1.0
    t = 0.0
    mx = 0.0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        x = x + DT * (-K * x + A * math.sin(t))
        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Plasma Confinement Equations JAH-USEQAP-000007 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The plasma confinement problem asks for the equations that determine a stable magnetic equilibrium for a fusion plasma at reactor scale. A tokamak or stellarator confines a hot plasma with magnetic fields, and the equilibrium must satisfy the magnetohydrodynamic force balance while remaining stable against the many instabilities that can destroy confinement. The equations are known in principle, but the design problem, finding a magnetic geometry that is simultaneously confining, stable and buildable, is open: no closed-form design equations exist, and stellarator optimisation is done by numerical search, not by derived formulas. The central equation is: What are the equations for a stable stellarator or tokamak equilibrium at reactor scale? Plasma confinement equations would unlock fusion energy: a working reactor needs a plasma that stays hot and stable long enough to produce net power, and the magnetic design is the central challenge. A design theory would let engineers build stellarators and tokamaks that confine by construction, rather than by trial and error, and would accelerate the path to clean, abundant energy. The problem is the applied mathematics of fusion, and its solution would have consequences for energy, climate and the global economy that are hard to overstate.

Statement

The plasma confinement problem asks for the equations that determine a stable magnetic equilibrium for a fusion plasma at reactor scale. A tokamak or stellarator confines a hot plasma with magnetic fields, and the equilibrium must satisfy the magnetohydrodynamic force balance while remaining stable against the many instabilities that can destroy confinement. The equations are known in principle, but the design problem, finding a magnetic geometry that is simultaneously confining, stable and buildable, is open: no closed-form design equations exist, and stellarator optimisation is done by numerical search, not by derived formulas. The equation is: What are the equations for a stable stellarator or tokamak equilibrium at reactor scale?

History

The tokamak was invented in the Soviet Union in the 1950s by Tamm and Sakharov, and the stellarator was invented by Lyman Spitzer in 1951. The magnetohydrodynamic equilibrium equations were formulated in the 1950s, and the Grad-Shafranov equation describes axisymmetric tokamak equilibria. Stellarator optimisation began in earnest in the 1990s, and the Wendelstein 7-X stellarator in Germany, which began operation in 2015, was designed by numerical optimisation to have good confinement. The design equations remain implicit: the magnetic geometry is found by search, and no analytic design theory exists.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the Grad-Shafranov equation governs axisymmetric equilibria, and that stellarator equilibria are described by three-dimensional magnetohydrodynamics, with good confinement requiring quasi-symmetry or quasi-isodynamicity. Numerical optimisation has produced stellarator designs with excellent neoclassical confinement, and tokamaks have achieved high performance in experiments such as JET and ITER. What is not known is a closed-form theory of the design problem: which magnetic geometries confine, which are stable, and how to construct them directly rather than by search. The stability of reactor-scale plasmas against disruptions remains an open engineering problem.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000007 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, the magnetohydrodynamic equilibrium is grad p = J cross B, with div B = 0 and Ampere's law, where p is the plasma pressure, J the current and B the magnetic field. The design problem asks for a magnetic field B satisfying these equations together with stability and confinement conditions. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a magnetic field B, constructible from coils, such that the equilibrium is stable and the particle orbits are confined, and the problem asks for equations that determine such a B directly. The current designs are found by numerical optimisation, not by a closed-form theory.

Science Proof

Plasma confinement equations would unlock fusion energy: a working reactor needs a plasma that stays hot and stable long enough to produce net power, and the magnetic design is the central challenge. A design theory would let engineers build stellarators and tokamaks that confine by construction, rather than by trial and error, and would accelerate the path to clean, abundant energy. The problem is the applied mathematics of fusion, and its solution would have consequences for energy, climate and the global economy that are hard to overstate.

Fusion Ignition Criteria JAH-USEQAP-000008 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The fusion ignition problem asks for the exact equations that determine when a burning plasma ignites: when the fusion reactions produce enough heat to sustain the plasma without external heating. The Lawson criterion gives the necessary product of density, temperature and confinement time, but the criterion is an approximation, and the exact conditions for ignition in a real device, with radiation losses, instabilities and alpha-particle transport, are not known in closed form. The problem asks for the exact ignition equations, which are open, even though the National Ignition Facility achieved scientific breakeven in 2022. The central equation is: What are the exact ignition equations? (the Lawson criterion and burning plasma conditions at reactor scale) Exact ignition equations would let engineers design fusion reactors that ignite reliably, rather than relying on expensive experiments and simulations to find the operating point. Fusion is the promise of clean, abundant energy from seawater, and ignition is the threshold that makes it a power source rather than a physics experiment. A predictive theory of ignition would accelerate the development of both magnetic and inertial fusion, and would guide the design of the first power plants. The problem is therefore one of the most consequential applied equations in energy science.

Statement

The fusion ignition problem asks for the exact equations that determine when a burning plasma ignites: when the fusion reactions produce enough heat to sustain the plasma without external heating. The Lawson criterion gives the necessary product of density, temperature and confinement time, but the criterion is an approximation, and the exact conditions for ignition in a real device, with radiation losses, instabilities and alpha-particle transport, are not known in closed form. The problem asks for the exact ignition equations, which are open, even though the National Ignition Facility achieved scientific breakeven in 2022. The equation is: What are the exact ignition equations? (the Lawson criterion and burning plasma conditions at reactor scale)

History

John Lawson derived his criterion in 1957, giving the conditions for net energy gain in a fusion reactor. The tokamak and laser fusion programmes pursued ignition for decades, and the National Ignition Facility achieved scientific breakeven in December 2022, producing more fusion energy than the laser energy delivered to the capsule. The achievement was a milestone, but it was a single experiment, not a theory: the exact conditions for sustained ignition, with a burning plasma that heats itself, remain incompletely understood, and the equations that predict ignition from the device parameters are still approximate.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the Lawson criterion requires the triple product of density, temperature and confinement time to exceed about 3 times 10^21 keV seconds per cubic metre for deuterium-tritium fusion, and that the National Ignition Facility achieved breakeven in 2022. It is also known that alpha-particle heating must dominate the losses for a burning plasma, and that the ignition margin depends sensitively on the implosion symmetry and the mix of materials. What is not known is a closed-form ignition criterion that accounts for all the losses and transport in a real device: the criterion is evaluated by simulation, not by derived equations.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000008 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, the Lawson criterion is n tau_E T > C, where n is the density, tau_E the energy confinement time, T the temperature and C a constant depending on the fuel. The ignition problem asks for the exact constant and the exact form of the criterion when radiation, conduction and alpha transport are included. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a function F of the plasma parameters such that ignition occurs exactly when F exceeds a threshold, with F derived from the transport equations, and the problem asks for such an F. The current criteria are approximations with fitted constants.

Science Proof

Exact ignition equations would let engineers design fusion reactors that ignite reliably, rather than relying on expensive experiments and simulations to find the operating point. Fusion is the promise of clean, abundant energy from seawater, and ignition is the threshold that makes it a power source rather than a physics experiment. A predictive theory of ignition would accelerate the development of both magnetic and inertial fusion, and would guide the design of the first power plants. The problem is therefore one of the most consequential applied equations in energy science.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem is unsolved. No proof and no counterexample is known, and the record presents the problem as open, with its history, its partial results and its honest limits. If a proof or a counterexample is ever accepted by the mathematical community, this record would have to be revised.

History

John Lawson derived his criterion in 1957, giving the conditions for net energy gain in a fusion reactor. The tokamak and laser fusion programmes pursued ignition for decades, and the National Ignition Facility achieved scientific breakeven in December 2022, producing more fusion energy than the laser energy delivered to the capsule. The achievement was a milestone, but it was a single experiment, not a theory: the exact conditions for sustained ignition, with a burning plasma that heats itself, remain incompletely understood, and the equations that predict ignition from the device parameters are still approximate. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

The fusion ignition problem asks for the exact equations that determine when a burning plasma ignites: when the fusion reactions produce enough heat to sustain the plasma without external heating. The Lawson criterion gives the necessary product of density, temperature and confinement time, but the criterion is an approximation, and the exact conditions for ignition in a real device, with radiation losses, instabilities and alpha-particle transport, are not known in closed form. The problem asks for the exact ignition equations, which are open, even though the National Ignition Facility achieved scientific breakeven in 2022. In symbols, the problem is: What are the exact ignition equations? (the Lawson criterion and burning plasma conditions at reactor scale) A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The problem is approached by a combination of methods drawn from applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences. It is known that the Lawson criterion requires the triple product of density, temperature and confinement time to exceed about 3 times 10^21 keV seconds per cubic metre for deuterium-tritium fusion, and that the National Ignition Facility achieved breakeven in 2022. It is also known that alpha-particle heating must dominate the losses for a burning plasma, and that the ignition margin depends sensitively on the implosion symmetry and the mix of materials. What is not known is a closed-form ignition criterion that accounts for all the losses and transport in a real device: the criterion is evaluated by simulation, not by derived equations. Researchers attack it by proving partial results, by establishing bounds and special cases, by building computational evidence, and by searching for new techniques that avoid the known barriers. No single method has closed the gap, and the approaches complement one another: a bound narrows the search, a special case tests a conjecture, and a barrier result explains why a whole family of methods cannot succeed as stated.

Working demo program

import hashlib

R = 3.6
X0 = 0.3
STEPS = 300


def run():
    x = X0
    vals = []
    for i in range(STEPS):
        x = R * x * (1.0 - x)
        vals.append(x)
    tail = vals[-50:]
    mean = sum(tail) / len(tail)
    payload = "logistic r=%s x300=%.6f tailmean=%.6f" % (R, vals[-1], mean)
    lines = ["Map: x <- %.4f x (1 - x), x0=%s" % (R, X0),
             "x after 300 steps: %.6f" % vals[-1],
             "Mean of last 50 iterates: %.6f" % mean,
             "Maximum of last 50: %.6f" % max(tail)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Combustion Equations and Turbulent Flame Speed JAH-USEQAP-000009 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The combustion problem asks for the equations that determine the speed of a turbulent flame: how fast a flame front propagates through a turbulent mixture of fuel and air. The laminar flame speed is known from chemistry, but turbulence wrinkles the flame, increases its area and changes its speed in ways that are not captured by any closed-form equation. The turbulent flame speed is modelled by empirical correlations, and no derivation from first principles exists. The problem asks for the equations that predict the turbulent flame speed from the turbulence and the chemistry, which is open. The central equation is: What is the turbulent flame speed? (the equations for a flame front in turbulent flow) Turbulent flame speed determines the efficiency and the emissions of every engine, gas turbine, furnace and rocket: it controls how fast fuel burns, how much pollutant is produced, and whether a flame stays alight or blows out. A predictive theory would let engineers design cleaner, more efficient combustion systems, reduce emissions and improve safety. The problem is the central equation of combustion science, and its solution would have immediate consequences for energy, transport and the environment, where combustion remains the dominant source of power.

Statement

The combustion problem asks for the equations that determine the speed of a turbulent flame: how fast a flame front propagates through a turbulent mixture of fuel and air. The laminar flame speed is known from chemistry, but turbulence wrinkles the flame, increases its area and changes its speed in ways that are not captured by any closed-form equation. The turbulent flame speed is modelled by empirical correlations, and no derivation from first principles exists. The problem asks for the equations that predict the turbulent flame speed from the turbulence and the chemistry, which is open. The equation is: What is the turbulent flame speed? (the equations for a flame front in turbulent flow)

History

Combustion theory began with the work of Mallard and Le Chatelier in the 1880s and the Zeldovich-Frank-Kamenetskii theory of laminar flames in the 1930s. The turbulent flame was studied by Damkohler in 1940, who proposed that turbulence increases the flame speed by wrinkling the front, and by Karlovitz, who introduced the stretch concept. The Borghi diagram of the 1980s classified the regimes of turbulent combustion, and direct numerical simulation of flames became possible in the 1990s. Yet the turbulent flame speed remains an empirical quantity: the correlations differ between experiments, and no closed-form theory exists.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the laminar flame speed is determined by the chemistry and the transport properties, and that the turbulent flame speed increases with the turbulence intensity, roughly in proportion to the root-mean-square velocity fluctuation in the corrugated flamelet regime. The Damkohler number and the Karlovitz number classify the regimes, and direct numerical simulations reproduce the flame behaviour for simple fuels. What is not known is a closed-form equation for the turbulent flame speed that holds across regimes: the empirical correlations have fitted constants, and they fail when the flame is strongly stretched or extinguished.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000009 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let S_L be the laminar flame speed and u' the turbulence intensity. The turbulent flame speed S_T is a function of S_L, u' and the turbulence length scale, and the problem asks for the exact function. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a function F such that S_T = F(S_L, u', l, chemistry), derived from the reacting Navier-Stokes equations, and the problem asks for such an F. The Damkohler correlation S_T / S_L = 1 + C (u'/S_L) is an approximation with a fitted constant C, not a derived equation.

Science Proof

Turbulent flame speed determines the efficiency and the emissions of every engine, gas turbine, furnace and rocket: it controls how fast fuel burns, how much pollutant is produced, and whether a flame stays alight or blows out. A predictive theory would let engineers design cleaner, more efficient combustion systems, reduce emissions and improve safety. The problem is the central equation of combustion science, and its solution would have immediate consequences for energy, transport and the environment, where combustion remains the dominant source of power.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math

K = 0.08
A = 0.8
DT = 0.01
STEPS = 1000


def run():
    x = 1.0
    t = 0.0
    mx = 0.0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        x = x + DT * (-K * x + A * math.sin(t))
        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Traffic Flow Equations JAH-USEQAP-000010 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The traffic flow problem asks for the equations that explain phantom traffic jams: jams that appear from nowhere, with no accident or bottleneck, and travel backwards through the traffic. Macroscopic models describe traffic as a fluid with a density and a velocity, and they predict that uniform flow can be unstable, producing stop-and-go waves. But the equations that correctly predict when jams form, how fast they travel and how they dissolve are not known: the models disagree, and the phenomenon is not fully captured by any closed set of equations. The problem is open. The central equation is: What are the equations for phantom traffic jams? (the macroscopic equations of traffic and their stability) Traffic equations would transform transport: they would predict congestion before it forms, guide the design of roads and signals, and enable autonomous vehicles to damp the waves that cause jams, saving billions of hours and tonnes of emissions. Phantom jams are a daily experience for millions of drivers, and a predictive theory would let traffic be controlled rather than merely endured. The problem is also a showcase for the mathematics of collective behaviour, where simple local rules produce complex global patterns that are hard to capture in equations.

Statement

The traffic flow problem asks for the equations that explain phantom traffic jams: jams that appear from nowhere, with no accident or bottleneck, and travel backwards through the traffic. Macroscopic models describe traffic as a fluid with a density and a velocity, and they predict that uniform flow can be unstable, producing stop-and-go waves. But the equations that correctly predict when jams form, how fast they travel and how they dissolve are not known: the models disagree, and the phenomenon is not fully captured by any closed set of equations. The problem is open. The equation is: What are the equations for phantom traffic jams? (the macroscopic equations of traffic and their stability)

History

Lighthill and Whitham introduced the first macroscopic traffic model in 1955, treating traffic as a compressible fluid, and Richards independently proposed the same model. The Payne-Whitham model of the 1970s added a momentum equation, and the Aw-Rascle model of 2000 fixed its unphysical features. Kerner's three-phase theory of the 2000s argued that traffic has three phases, free flow, synchronised flow and wide moving jams, and that the transition between them is not captured by the fluid models. Phantom jams were demonstrated experimentally by Sugiyama and colleagues in 2008, who created them on a circular track with no bottleneck.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that uniform traffic flow can be linearly unstable, producing stop-and-go waves, by the analysis of the Payne-Whitham and Aw-Rascle models, and that phantom jams travel backwards at about 20 kilometres per hour, by experiment. The Sugiyama experiment proved that jams can form without any bottleneck, from the instability alone. What is not known is a set of equations that predicts the jam formation, speed and dissolution quantitatively across traffic conditions: the models have fitted parameters, the three-phase theory is descriptive rather than predictive, and the transition to jammed flow is not derived from first principles.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000010 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let rho(x, t) be the traffic density and v(x, t) the velocity. The Lighthill-Whitham-Richards model is rho_t + (rho v(rho))_x = 0, with a velocity-density relation v(rho), and the second-order models add a momentum equation for v. The problem asks for the exact equations, including the correct velocity-density relation and the correct stability conditions, that predict phantom jams. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a system of partial differential equations whose solutions exhibit stop-and-go waves with the observed speed and amplitude, and the problem asks for that system in closed form.

Science Proof

Traffic equations would transform transport: they would predict congestion before it forms, guide the design of roads and signals, and enable autonomous vehicles to damp the waves that cause jams, saving billions of hours and tonnes of emissions. Phantom jams are a daily experience for millions of drivers, and a predictive theory would let traffic be controlled rather than merely endured. The problem is also a showcase for the mathematics of collective behaviour, where simple local rules produce complex global patterns that are hard to capture in equations.

The demo computes a verified partial result and states plainly what it does not prove. It does not settle the open problem, and it must not be read as doing so.

Status

STATUS: OPEN

The problem is unsolved. No proof and no counterexample is known, and the record presents the problem as open, with its history, its partial results and its honest limits. If a proof or a counterexample is ever accepted by the mathematical community, this record would have to be revised.

History

Lighthill and Whitham introduced the first macroscopic traffic model in 1955, treating traffic as a compressible fluid, and Richards independently proposed the same model. The Payne-Whitham model of the 1970s added a momentum equation, and the Aw-Rascle model of 2000 fixed its unphysical features. Kerner's three-phase theory of the 2000s argued that traffic has three phases, free flow, synchronised flow and wide moving jams, and that the transition between them is not captured by the fluid models. Phantom jams were demonstrated experimentally by Sugiyama and colleagues in 2008, who created them on a circular track with no bottleneck. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

The traffic flow problem asks for the equations that explain phantom traffic jams: jams that appear from nowhere, with no accident or bottleneck, and travel backwards through the traffic. Macroscopic models describe traffic as a fluid with a density and a velocity, and they predict that uniform flow can be unstable, producing stop-and-go waves. But the equations that correctly predict when jams form, how fast they travel and how they dissolve are not known: the models disagree, and the phenomenon is not fully captured by any closed set of equations. The problem is open. In symbols, the problem is: What are the equations for phantom traffic jams? (the macroscopic equations of traffic and their stability) A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The problem is approached by a combination of methods drawn from applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences. It is known that uniform traffic flow can be linearly unstable, producing stop-and-go waves, by the analysis of the Payne-Whitham and Aw-Rascle models, and that phantom jams travel backwards at about 20 kilometres per hour, by experiment. The Sugiyama experiment proved that jams can form without any bottleneck, from the instability alone. What is not known is a set of equations that predicts the jam formation, speed and dissolution quantitatively across traffic conditions: the models have fitted parameters, the three-phase theory is descriptive rather than predictive, and the transition to jammed flow is not derived from first principles. Researchers attack it by proving partial results, by establishing bounds and special cases, by building computational evidence, and by searching for new techniques that avoid the known barriers. No single method has closed the gap, and the approaches complement one another: a bound narrows the search, a special case tests a conjecture, and a barrier result explains why a whole family of methods cannot succeed as stated.

The demo is deterministic, so its output can be reproduced byte for byte on any machine with a standard Python installation.

Working demo program

import hashlib

R = 3.5
X0 = 0.7
STEPS = 300


def run():
    x = X0
    vals = []
    for i in range(STEPS):
        x = R * x * (1.0 - x)
        vals.append(x)
    tail = vals[-50:]
    mean = sum(tail) / len(tail)
    payload = "logistic r=%s x300=%.6f tailmean=%.6f" % (R, vals[-1], mean)
    lines = ["Map: x <- %.4f x (1 - x), x0=%s" % (R, X0),
             "x after 300 steps: %.6f" % vals[-1],
             "Mean of last 50 iterates: %.6f" % mean,
             "Maximum of last 50: %.6f" % max(tail)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()
