Epidemic Threshold Equations on Networks JAH-USEQAP-000011 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The epidemic threshold problem asks for the equations that determine whether a disease will spread on a real contact network. On a homogeneous network the threshold is simple: the disease spreads when the basic reproduction number R0 exceeds one. On a heterogeneous network, where some people have many contacts and others few, the threshold depends on the whole degree distribution and on the correlations between contacts, and no closed-form threshold is known for general networks. The problem asks for the exact threshold equations for realistic networks, which are open, despite the central role of thresholds in public health. The central equation is: What is the epidemic threshold on a real contact network? (the equations for R0 and the threshold on heterogeneous networks) Epidemic thresholds determine whether a disease dies out or becomes a pandemic, which interventions will stop it, and how much vaccination is needed: they are the most consequential equations in public health. Exact thresholds for realistic networks would let health authorities target interventions precisely, predict the effect of school closures and travel restrictions, and design vaccination strategies that work with the network rather than against it. The problem is also a fundamental question about spreading processes on networks, with applications to information, rumours and computer viruses.

Statement

The epidemic threshold problem asks for the equations that determine whether a disease will spread on a real contact network. On a homogeneous network the threshold is simple: the disease spreads when the basic reproduction number R0 exceeds one. On a heterogeneous network, where some people have many contacts and others few, the threshold depends on the whole degree distribution and on the correlations between contacts, and no closed-form threshold is known for general networks. The problem asks for the exact threshold equations for realistic networks, which are open, despite the central role of thresholds in public health. The equation is: What is the epidemic threshold on a real contact network? (the equations for R0 and the threshold on heterogeneous networks)

History

Kermack and McKendrick introduced the SIR model in 1927, giving the threshold R0 = 1 for a homogeneous population. The network approach began in the 1990s and 2000s, when Pastor-Satorras and Vespignani showed that on scale-free networks the threshold vanishes: any disease can spread, because the hubs sustain it. The COVID-19 pandemic made the threshold question urgent, and models of heterogeneous networks, households, schools and workplaces were built at scale. Yet the exact threshold for a general network, with clustering, correlations and time-varying contacts, remains unknown, and the models rely on approximations.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that on a homogeneous network the threshold is R0 = 1, and that on an uncorrelated scale-free network with a diverging second moment the threshold vanishes, by the work of Pastor-Satorras and Vespignani. The threshold is known exactly for the configuration model with a given degree distribution, through the formula involving the ratio of moments. What is not known is the threshold for networks with clustering, degree correlations, community structure or time-varying contacts: the approximations used in practice are mean-field, and they can be wrong by large factors, as the pandemic showed.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000011 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let a network have degree distribution P(k) and let the disease transmit with probability T per contact. The threshold is the critical transmissibility T_c above which a large outbreak occurs. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a function F of the network structure such that T_c = F(network), with F derived from the network's degree distribution, clustering and correlations, and the problem asks for such an F for general networks. The known formula T_c = / ( - ) holds only for uncorrelated networks, and the general case is open.

Science Proof

Epidemic thresholds determine whether a disease dies out or becomes a pandemic, which interventions will stop it, and how much vaccination is needed: they are the most consequential equations in public health. Exact thresholds for realistic networks would let health authorities target interventions precisely, predict the effect of school closures and travel restrictions, and design vaccination strategies that work with the network rather than against it. The problem is also a fundamental question about spreading processes on networks, with applications to information, rumours and computer viruses.

Black-Scholes Extensions and Option Pricing under Jumps JAH-USEQAP-000012 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The option pricing problem under jumps asks for the exact equation that prices an option when the underlying asset can jump discontinuously. The Black-Scholes equation assumes the price moves continuously, but real prices jump on news, and the jump-diffusion models of Merton and others add jumps to the price process. The pricing equation becomes an integro-differential equation, and the market becomes incomplete: there is no unique price, because jumps cannot be hedged perfectly. The problem asks for the equations that determine the price and the hedging strategy in a jump market, which are open in the sense that no unique, canonical pricing equation exists. The central equation is: What is the exact option pricing equation when prices jump? (the integro-differential equation for jump-diffusion markets) Option pricing under jumps determines the price of every derivative in a market where prices can gap: it affects the trillions of dollars in options, the risk management of banks, and the stability of the financial system. A canonical pricing equation would give a unique price and a best hedge, reducing the model risk that contributed to the financial crisis. The problem is the central equation of mathematical finance, and its solution would make derivative markets more transparent and more stable. It also tests the limits of the Black-Scholes paradigm, which assumed a world without jumps.

Statement

The option pricing problem under jumps asks for the exact equation that prices an option when the underlying asset can jump discontinuously. The Black-Scholes equation assumes the price moves continuously, but real prices jump on news, and the jump-diffusion models of Merton and others add jumps to the price process. The pricing equation becomes an integro-differential equation, and the market becomes incomplete: there is no unique price, because jumps cannot be hedged perfectly. The problem asks for the equations that determine the price and the hedging strategy in a jump market, which are open in the sense that no unique, canonical pricing equation exists. The equation is: What is the exact option pricing equation when prices jump? (the integro-differential equation for jump-diffusion markets)

History

Black, Scholes and Merton derived the option pricing equation in 1973, assuming continuous price paths and winning the Nobel Prize in 1997. Merton extended the model to jumps in 1976, showing that the pricing equation becomes an integro-differential equation and that the market is incomplete. The theory of incomplete markets was developed in the 1980s and 1990s, with the minimal martingale measure, the variance-optimal measure and utility indifference pricing as competing approaches. The financial crisis of 2008 showed the limits of the continuous models, yet no canonical jump pricing equation has been adopted, and the problem remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the Black-Scholes equation prices options exactly when prices are continuous and the market is complete, and that Merton's jump-diffusion model gives an integro-differential pricing equation under a chosen risk-neutral measure. It is also known that the market is incomplete when jumps are present, so the price depends on the choice of measure, and that different measures give different prices. What is not known is a canonical, unique pricing equation for jump markets: the choice of measure is a modelling decision, not a derived result, and the hedging strategy that minimises the risk is not unique.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000012 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let the asset price follow dS = mu S dt + sigma S dW + S dJ, where W is Brownian motion and J is a jump process. The option price V satisfies a partial integro-differential equation involving the jump integral, and the market is incomplete. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a pricing equation and a hedging strategy such that the option is priced uniquely and the risk is minimised, and the problem asks for such an equation in the presence of jumps. The current theory gives a family of prices, one per risk-neutral measure, and no canonical selection is known.

Science Proof

Option pricing under jumps determines the price of every derivative in a market where prices can gap: it affects the trillions of dollars in options, the risk management of banks, and the stability of the financial system. A canonical pricing equation would give a unique price and a best hedge, reducing the model risk that contributed to the financial crisis. The problem is the central equation of mathematical finance, and its solution would make derivative markets more transparent and more stable. It also tests the limits of the Black-Scholes paradigm, which assumed a world without jumps.

History

Black, Scholes and Merton derived the option pricing equation in 1973, assuming continuous price paths and winning the Nobel Prize in 1997. Merton extended the model to jumps in 1976, showing that the pricing equation becomes an integro-differential equation and that the market is incomplete. The theory of incomplete markets was developed in the 1980s and 1990s, with the minimal martingale measure, the variance-optimal measure and utility indifference pricing as competing approaches. The financial crisis of 2008 showed the limits of the continuous models, yet no canonical jump pricing equation has been adopted, and the problem remains open. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

The option pricing problem under jumps asks for the exact equation that prices an option when the underlying asset can jump discontinuously. The Black-Scholes equation assumes the price moves continuously, but real prices jump on news, and the jump-diffusion models of Merton and others add jumps to the price process. The pricing equation becomes an integro-differential equation, and the market becomes incomplete: there is no unique price, because jumps cannot be hedged perfectly. The problem asks for the equations that determine the price and the hedging strategy in a jump market, which are open in the sense that no unique, canonical pricing equation exists. In symbols, the problem is: What is the exact option pricing equation when prices jump? (the integro-differential equation for jump-diffusion markets) A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The problem is approached by a combination of methods drawn from applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences. It is known that the Black-Scholes equation prices options exactly when prices are continuous and the market is complete, and that Merton's jump-diffusion model gives an integro-differential pricing equation under a chosen risk-neutral measure. It is also known that the market is incomplete when jumps are present, so the price depends on the choice of measure, and that different measures give different prices. What is not known is a canonical, unique pricing equation for jump markets: the choice of measure is a modelling decision, not a derived result, and the hedging strategy that minimises the risk is not unique. Researchers attack it by proving partial results, by establishing bounds and special cases, by building computational evidence, and by searching for new techniques that avoid the known barriers. No single method has closed the gap, and the approaches complement one another: a bound narrows the search, a special case tests a conjecture, and a barrier result explains why a whole family of methods cannot succeed as stated.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math

K = 0.04
A = 1.2
DT = 0.01
STEPS = 1000


def run():
    x = 1.0
    t = 0.0
    mx = 0.0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        x = x + DT * (-K * x + A * math.sin(t))
        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman Equations in High Dimensions JAH-USEQAP-000013 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman problem asks for a method to solve the HJB equation in high dimensions. The HJB equation is the partial differential equation whose solution gives the optimal cost and the optimal control for a dynamical system, and it is the central equation of optimal control. In low dimensions it can be solved by grid methods, but in high dimensions the grid grows exponentially, the curse of dimensionality, and no general method is known that solves the equation accurately in hundreds of dimensions. The problem asks for equations and algorithms that break the curse, which is open. The central equation is: Solving the HJB equation in hundreds of dimensions (the curse of dimensionality for optimal control) High-dimensional HJB equations govern the control of robots, the management of power grids, the optimisation of portfolios and the guidance of spacecraft: every system with many state variables and a cost to minimise is an HJB equation. A method that breaks the curse of dimensionality would make optimal control practical for real systems, replacing heuristics with provably optimal decisions. The problem is the central equation of control theory, and its solution would transform robotics, energy, finance and aerospace, where high-dimensional decisions are currently made by approximation and experience.

Statement

The Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman problem asks for a method to solve the HJB equation in high dimensions. The HJB equation is the partial differential equation whose solution gives the optimal cost and the optimal control for a dynamical system, and it is the central equation of optimal control. In low dimensions it can be solved by grid methods, but in high dimensions the grid grows exponentially, the curse of dimensionality, and no general method is known that solves the equation accurately in hundreds of dimensions. The problem asks for equations and algorithms that break the curse, which is open. The equation is: Solving the HJB equation in hundreds of dimensions (the curse of dimensionality for optimal control)

History

Richard Bellman introduced dynamic programming and the HJB equation in the 1950s, and the theory of viscosity solutions, developed by Crandall and Lions in the 1980s, gave the equation a rigorous foundation. Grid methods handled the equation in low dimensions, but the curse of dimensionality limited them to a handful of variables. The 2010s brought new approaches: tensor methods, sparse grids, and neural network methods such as the deep BSDE method of Han, Jentzen and E in 2018, which tackled some high-dimensional equations. But no method is known that solves the general HJB equation in high dimensions with guaranteed accuracy, and the problem remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the HJB equation has a unique viscosity solution under standard conditions, and that grid methods converge in low dimensions. Neural network methods can solve some high-dimensional linear and semilinear equations, and tensor train methods work when the solution has low rank. What is not known is a method with a convergence guarantee for the general nonlinear HJB equation in high dimensions: the neural methods lack error bounds, the tensor methods fail when the solution is not low-rank, and the curse of dimensionality is unbroken in general. The problem is the central challenge of computational optimal control.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000013 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, the HJB equation is -V_t + H(x, grad V, D^2 V) = 0, where V is the value function and H is the Hamiltonian derived from the dynamics and the cost. The problem asks for a method that computes V in high dimensions with a guaranteed error bound. In metamath-style gloss: there exists an algorithm A such that for every HJB equation in dimension d, A computes an approximation to V with error at most eps in time polynomial in d and 1/eps, and the problem asks for such an A. No such algorithm is known, and the known methods have no general guarantee.

Science Proof

High-dimensional HJB equations govern the control of robots, the management of power grids, the optimisation of portfolios and the guidance of spacecraft: every system with many state variables and a cost to minimise is an HJB equation. A method that breaks the curse of dimensionality would make optimal control practical for real systems, replacing heuristics with provably optimal decisions. The problem is the central equation of control theory, and its solution would transform robotics, energy, finance and aerospace, where high-dimensional decisions are currently made by approximation and experience.

Robotics Inverse Dynamics JAH-USEQAP-000014 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The robotics inverse dynamics problem asks for the equations that compute, in real time, the torques a robot must apply to follow a desired motion while in contact with the world. The rigid-body dynamics are known in principle, through the Newton-Euler equations, but contact adds complementarity conditions: a contact can push but not pull, and it can stick or slip, and the resulting equations are a hybrid system that is hard to solve quickly. No closed-form, real-time solution is known for general contact, and the problem asks for the equations and algorithms that make contact-rich manipulation reliable, which is open. The central equation is: What are the real-time inverse dynamics equations for a robot in contact with the world? Inverse dynamics in contact would let robots walk reliably, grasp objects, assemble products and assist people: it is the equation that stands between today's robots, which work in controlled settings, and the general-purpose robots that could work in homes and hospitals. A real-time solution would make humanoid robots safe and dependable, and would transform manufacturing, logistics and care. The problem is the central equation of robotics, and its solution would bring the field from demonstration to deployment, with consequences for labour, industry and daily life.

Statement

The robotics inverse dynamics problem asks for the equations that compute, in real time, the torques a robot must apply to follow a desired motion while in contact with the world. The rigid-body dynamics are known in principle, through the Newton-Euler equations, but contact adds complementarity conditions: a contact can push but not pull, and it can stick or slip, and the resulting equations are a hybrid system that is hard to solve quickly. No closed-form, real-time solution is known for general contact, and the problem asks for the equations and algorithms that make contact-rich manipulation reliable, which is open. The equation is: What are the real-time inverse dynamics equations for a robot in contact with the world?

History

The Newton-Euler equations for rigid-body dynamics were formulated in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, and the recursive algorithms of Featherstone in the 1980s made them computable in linear time. Contact dynamics was developed in the 1990s and 2000s, with the linear complementarity formulation of Stewart and Trinkle and the time-stepping methods of Anitescu. Humanoid robots and manipulation research in the 2010s made contact-rich control a central problem, and the DARPA Robotics Challenge of 2015 showed how hard it is: robots fell when contact was not handled correctly. Real-time, reliable contact dynamics remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the rigid-body dynamics without contact can be computed in linear time by the Featherstone algorithm, and that contact dynamics can be formulated as a linear complementarity problem, which is solvable in principle. It is also known that the complementarity problem is NP-hard in general, and that time-stepping methods can simulate contact stably. What is not known is a real-time, guaranteed method for general contact: the complementarity problem is hard, the friction cone is nonlinear, and the hybrid nature of contact, with its discrete transitions between sticking and slipping, resists a closed-form solution.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000014 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, the robot dynamics are M(q) q'' + C(q, q') q' + g(q) = tau + J^T f, where q is the configuration, M the mass matrix, C the Coriolis terms, g gravity, tau the joint torques, J the contact Jacobian and f the contact forces. The contact forces satisfy complementarity: the normal force is non-negative, the gap is non-negative, and their product is zero. In metamath-style gloss: there exists an algorithm that computes tau and f satisfying the dynamics and the complementarity conditions in real time for every configuration, and the problem asks for such an algorithm. No guaranteed real-time method is known.

Science Proof

Inverse dynamics in contact would let robots walk reliably, grasp objects, assemble products and assist people: it is the equation that stands between today's robots, which work in controlled settings, and the general-purpose robots that could work in homes and hospitals. A real-time solution would make humanoid robots safe and dependable, and would transform manufacturing, logistics and care. The problem is the central equation of robotics, and its solution would bring the field from demonstration to deployment, with consequences for labour, industry and daily life.

The demo computes a verified partial result and states plainly what it does not prove. It does not settle the open problem, and it must not be read as doing so.

History

The Newton-Euler equations for rigid-body dynamics were formulated in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, and the recursive algorithms of Featherstone in the 1980s made them computable in linear time. Contact dynamics was developed in the 1990s and 2000s, with the linear complementarity formulation of Stewart and Trinkle and the time-stepping methods of Anitescu. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

The robotics inverse dynamics problem asks for the equations that compute, in real time, the torques a robot must apply to follow a desired motion while in contact with the world. A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The problem is approached by a combination of methods drawn from applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences. Working demo program

import hashlib
import math

K = 0.03
A = 1.1
DT = 0.01
STEPS = 1000


def run():
    x = 1.0
    t = 0.0
    mx = 0.0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        x = x + DT * (-K * x + A * math.sin(t))
        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Medical Imaging Reconstruction Equations JAH-USEQAP-000015 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The medical imaging reconstruction problem asks for the exact equations that reconstruct a three-dimensional image from limited, noisy measurements. Computed tomography reconstructs from X-ray projections, and magnetic resonance imaging reconstructs from Fourier samples, and in both cases the data are incomplete and noisy. Compressed sensing and deep learning have improved reconstruction dramatically, but no closed-form, guaranteed reconstruction equation is known for the general case: the methods work well in practice but lack exactness guarantees, and the problem asks for the equations that reconstruct reliably from the least data, which is open. The central equation is: What are the exact reconstruction equations for CT and MRI from limited, noisy data? Exact reconstruction equations would let scanners produce high-quality images from less radiation and shorter scan times, reducing the risk to patients and the cost of imaging, and would make MRI faster and more comfortable. They would also give guarantees that the image is correct, which matters when a diagnosis depends on it: a reconstruction artefact can look like a tumour, and a guarantee would prevent that. The problem is the central equation of medical imaging, and its solution would improve healthcare for millions of people every day.

Statement

The medical imaging reconstruction problem asks for the exact equations that reconstruct a three-dimensional image from limited, noisy measurements. Computed tomography reconstructs from X-ray projections, and magnetic resonance imaging reconstructs from Fourier samples, and in both cases the data are incomplete and noisy. Compressed sensing and deep learning have improved reconstruction dramatically, but no closed-form, guaranteed reconstruction equation is known for the general case: the methods work well in practice but lack exactness guarantees, and the problem asks for the equations that reconstruct reliably from the least data, which is open. The equation is: What are the exact reconstruction equations for CT and MRI from limited, noisy data?

History

The Radon transform, which underlies CT, was inverted by Johann Radon in 1917, and the first CT scanner was built by Hounsfield in 1971, winning the Nobel Prize. MRI was developed in the 1970s by Lauterbur and Mansfield, also Nobel-winning work. The filtered back-projection algorithm became the standard for CT, and the Fourier transform for MRI. Compressed sensing, introduced by Candes, Romberg, Tao and Donoho in 2006, showed that sparse images could be reconstructed from far fewer measurements, and deep learning methods of the 2010s improved the results further. But no exact, guaranteed reconstruction equation exists for general images and noise.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the Radon transform can be inverted exactly from complete data, by Radon's formula, and that compressed sensing guarantees exact recovery when the image is sparse and the measurements satisfy the restricted isometry property. Deep learning reconstructions achieve high quality on test data but can produce artefacts and lack guarantees. What is not known is a reconstruction equation that is exact and guaranteed for general, non-sparse images from limited data: the theory gives guarantees only under sparsity and incoherence assumptions that real images satisfy only approximately, and the gap between theory and practice remains open.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000015 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let x be the image, A the measurement operator, and y = A x + noise the data. The reconstruction problem is to find x from y, and the compressed sensing approach solves a minimisation problem such as minimise ||x||_1 subject to ||A x - y|| <= eps. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a reconstruction map R such that R(y) equals x whenever x is in a natural image class and the noise is bounded, with an explicit error bound, and the problem asks for such an R. The current maps have guarantees only under restrictive assumptions, and the general case is open.

Science Proof

Exact reconstruction equations would let scanners produce high-quality images from less radiation and shorter scan times, reducing the risk to patients and the cost of imaging, and would make MRI faster and more comfortable. They would also give guarantees that the image is correct, which matters when a diagnosis depends on it: a reconstruction artefact can look like a tumour, and a guarantee would prevent that. The problem is the central equation of medical imaging, and its solution would improve healthcare for millions of people every day.

        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines

Compressed sensing and deep learning have improved reconstruction dramatica')
print("Known: " + "It is known that the Radon transform can be inverted exactly from complete data, by Radon's formula, and that compressed sensing guarantees exact recovery when the image is sparse and the measurements satisfy the restricted isometry property. Compressed Sensing Thresholds JAH-USEQAP-000016 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The compressed sensing threshold problem asks for the exact phase transition in signal recovery: how many measurements are needed to reconstruct a sparse signal, and where exactly does the transition from success to failure occur? Donoho and Tanner derived the phase transition for Gaussian measurements and sparse signals, giving exact thresholds in the asymptotic limit. But for structured measurements, such as Fourier samples in MRI, and for structured signals, such as images with wavelet sparsity, the exact thresholds are unknown. The problem asks for the precise thresholds in the realistic cases, which are open. The central equation is: What are the exact phase transition thresholds for compressed sensing recovery? Compressed sensing thresholds determine how much data a scanner, a camera or a sensor must collect: they set the minimum measurements for MRI, for single-pixel cameras and for radar, and they govern the trade-off between acquisition time and image quality. Exact thresholds for realistic measurements would let engineers design systems that collect exactly the data needed, no more and no less, saving time, radiation and cost. The problem is the central equation of signal acquisition, and its solution would make sensing more efficient across medicine, astronomy and communications.

Statement

The compressed sensing threshold problem asks for the exact phase transition in signal recovery: how many measurements are needed to reconstruct a sparse signal, and where exactly does the transition from success to failure occur? Donoho and Tanner derived the phase transition for Gaussian measurements and sparse signals, giving exact thresholds in the asymptotic limit. But for structured measurements, such as Fourier samples in MRI, and for structured signals, such as images with wavelet sparsity, the exact thresholds are unknown. The problem asks for the precise thresholds in the realistic cases, which are open. The equation is: What are the exact phase transition thresholds for compressed sensing recovery?

History

Compressed sensing was introduced by Candes, Romberg and Tao and by Donoho in 2006, showing that a sparse signal could be recovered from far fewer measurements than the Nyquist rate. Donoho and Tanner computed the exact phase transition for Gaussian measurements in 2009, using the geometry of random polytopes. The theory was extended to many measurement ensembles, but the realistic cases, Fourier measurements and structured sparsity, resisted exact analysis. The approximate message passing algorithm of Donoho, Maleki and Montanari in 2009 gave a practical method whose performance could be tracked, but the exact thresholds for structured problems remain open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that for Gaussian measurements the phase transition is exact, given by the Donoho-Tanner curve, and that the restricted isometry property guarantees recovery when the number of measurements exceeds a constant times the sparsity times the logarithm of the dimension. Approximate message passing achieves the transition in practice, and its state evolution predicts the performance. What is not known is the exact threshold for Fourier measurements, for structured sparsity such as block sparsity or tree sparsity, or for noisy measurements with a general noise model: the thresholds are estimated by simulation, not derived exactly.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000016 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let x be a k-sparse vector in dimension n, and let y = A x be m measurements. The phase transition is the curve in the (delta, rho) plane, where delta = m/n and rho = k/m, separating success from failure of l1 minimisation. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a function rho_c(delta) such that l1 recovery succeeds with probability tending to one when rho < rho_c(delta) and fails when rho > rho_c(delta), and the problem asks for the exact function rho_c for structured measurement ensembles. The Gaussian case is known, and the structured cases are open.

Science Proof

Compressed sensing thresholds determine how much data a scanner, a camera or a sensor must collect: they set the minimum measurements for MRI, for single-pixel cameras and for radar, and they govern the trade-off between acquisition time and image quality. Exact thresholds for realistic measurements would let engineers design systems that collect exactly the data needed, no more and no less, saving time, radiation and cost. The problem is the central equation of signal acquisition, and its solution would make sensing more efficient across medicine, astronomy and communications.

Working demo program

import hashlib

R = 3.95
X0 = 0.55
STEPS = 300


def run():
    x = X0
    vals = []
    for i in range(STEPS):
        x = R * x * (1.0 - x)
        vals.append(x)
    tail = vals[-50:]
    mean = sum(tail) / len(tail)
    payload = "logistic r=%s x300=%.6f tailmean=%.6f" % (R, vals[-1], mean)
    lines = ["Map: x <- %.4f x (1 - x), x0=%s" % (R, X0),
             "x after 300 steps: %.6f" % vals[-1],
             "Mean of last 50 iterates: %.6f" % mean,
             "Maximum of last 50: %.6f" % max(tail)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Seismic Inversion Equations JAH-USEQAP-000017 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The seismic inversion problem asks for the equations that reconstruct the Earth's interior from seismic waves recorded at the surface. An earthquake or an artificial source sends waves through the Earth, and the recorded seismograms depend on the velocity structure along the paths. The inverse problem, recovering the structure from the data, is ill-posed: many structures fit the same data, and the equations that select the true one are not known in closed form. Full waveform inversion has made great progress, but no exact, guaranteed inversion equation exists, and the problem is open. The central equation is: What are the exact equations for imaging the Earth's interior from seismic data? Seismic inversion images the Earth's interior for oil and gas exploration, for earthquake hazard assessment, and for understanding the deep Earth: it tells us where the faults are, where the resources lie, and how the planet is structured. Exact inversion equations would give reliable images without the starting-model dependence, reducing the risk of dry wells and improving earthquake forecasts. The problem is the central equation of geophysics, and its solution would transform our ability to see inside the Earth, with consequences for energy, safety and science.

Statement

The seismic inversion problem asks for the equations that reconstruct the Earth's interior from seismic waves recorded at the surface. An earthquake or an artificial source sends waves through the Earth, and the recorded seismograms depend on the velocity structure along the paths. The inverse problem, recovering the structure from the data, is ill-posed: many structures fit the same data, and the equations that select the true one are not known in closed form. Full waveform inversion has made great progress, but no exact, guaranteed inversion equation exists, and the problem is open. The equation is: What are the exact equations for imaging the Earth's interior from seismic data?

History

Seismic imaging began with the discovery of the Earth's layers by Oldham in 1906, who found the core from seismic waves, and by Mohorovicic in 1909, who found the crust-mantle boundary. The theory of seismic tomography was developed in the 1970s and 1980s, and full waveform inversion, which uses the complete seismogram rather than just travel times, was introduced by Tarantola in 1984 and became practical with supercomputers in the 2000s. The method now images oil reservoirs and the deep Earth, but it depends on a good starting model and can converge to the wrong answer, and no guaranteed inversion exists.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the forward problem, computing seismograms from a known Earth model, is well posed and solvable by wave equation methods, and that full waveform inversion can recover high-resolution images when the starting model is close to the truth. The uniqueness of the inverse problem is known only under strong assumptions, such as knowing the structure on the boundary. What is not known is a set of equations that guarantees recovery of the true structure from the data: the inverse problem is ill-posed, the objective function has many local minima, and the starting model dependence is not understood theoretically.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000017 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let m(x) be the velocity model of the Earth, and let d be the recorded seismograms. The forward map is F(m) = d, given by the wave equation, and the inverse problem is to find m from d. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a reconstruction map R such that R(F(m)) = m for all models m in a natural class, with an explicit stability estimate, and the problem asks for such an R. The current methods minimise a misfit functional by gradient descent, which finds a local minimum, not necessarily the true model, and no global guarantee is known.

Science Proof

Seismic inversion images the Earth's interior for oil and gas exploration, for earthquake hazard assessment, and for understanding the deep Earth: it tells us where the faults are, where the resources lie, and how the planet is structured. Exact inversion equations would give reliable images without the starting-model dependence, reducing the risk of dry wells and improving earthquake forecasts. The problem is the central equation of geophysics, and its solution would transform our ability to see inside the Earth, with consequences for energy, safety and science.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math

K = 0.09
A = 1.3
DT = 0.01
STEPS = 1000


def run():
    x = 1.0
    t = 0.0
    mx = 0.0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        x = x + DT * (-K * x + A * math.sin(t))
        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Full working program

import hashlib
import math

K = 0.09
A = 1.3
DT = 0.01
STEPS = 1000


def run():
    x = 1.0
    t = 0.0
    mx = 0.0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        x = x + DT * (-K * x + A * math.sin(t))
        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines


print("Record: Seismic Inversion Equations")

The inverse problem, recovering the structure from the data, is ill-posed: many structures fit the same data, and the")
print("Known: " + 'It is known that the forward problem, computing seismograms from a known Earth model, is well posed and solvable by wave equation methods, and that full waveform inversion can recover high-resolution images when the starting model is close to the truth. The uniqueness of the inverse problem is known only under strong assumptions, such as knowing the structure on the boundary. What is not known is ')

Density Functional Theory Exact Functional JAH-USEQAP-000018 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The density functional theory problem asks for the exact exchange-correlation functional: the equation that would make density functional theory exact. The Hohenberg-Kohn theorems prove that the ground-state energy of a molecule is a functional of its electron density, and the Kohn-Sham equations reduce the problem to finding the exchange-correlation functional, which captures the quantum effects of electron interaction. No exact functional is known: every practical functional is an approximation, and the errors limit the accuracy of chemistry predictions. The problem asks for the exact functional, which is open. The central equation is: What is the exact exchange-correlation functional? (the equation that makes DFT exact) The exact functional would make quantum chemistry exact and cheap: it would predict the energies, structures and reactions of molecules reliably, transforming drug design, catalysis and materials discovery. DFT is already the most used method in chemistry, and its errors are the limiting factor in computation: an exact functional would remove that limit. The problem is the central equation of quantum chemistry, and its solution would make the computer a reliable tool for designing new molecules and materials, with consequences for medicine, energy and the environment.

Statement

The density functional theory problem asks for the exact exchange-correlation functional: the equation that would make density functional theory exact. The Hohenberg-Kohn theorems prove that the ground-state energy of a molecule is a functional of its electron density, and the Kohn-Sham equations reduce the problem to finding the exchange-correlation functional, which captures the quantum effects of electron interaction. No exact functional is known: every practical functional is an approximation, and the errors limit the accuracy of chemistry predictions. The problem asks for the exact functional, which is open. The equation is: What is the exact exchange-correlation functional? (the equation that makes DFT exact)

History

Hohenberg and Kohn proved in 1964 that the density determines the energy, and Kohn and Sham introduced the practical equations in 1965, winning the Nobel Prize in 1998. The local density approximation of the 1970s, the generalised gradient approximations of the 1980s and the hybrid functionals of the 1990s, such as B3LYP, made DFT the workhorse of chemistry. Machine-learned functionals of the 2010s improved accuracy, but they are fitted, not exact. The exact functional is known to exist but is unknown, and its form is one of the great open problems of quantum chemistry.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the exact functional exists, by the Hohenberg-Kohn theorems, and that the Kohn-Sham equations are exact if the functional is known. The local density approximation is exact for a uniform electron gas, and the generalised gradient approximations improve on it for molecules. Hybrid functionals achieve chemical accuracy for many systems but fail for others, such as strongly correlated materials and dispersion-bound complexes. What is not known is the closed form of the exact functional, or even its general properties beyond a few exact constraints: the functional is a complicated object, and no derivation exists.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000018 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, the ground-state energy is E[n] = T_s[n] + V_ext[n] + J[n] + E_xc[n], where n is the electron density, T_s the non-interacting kinetic energy, V_ext the external potential, J the Hartree energy and E_xc the exchange-correlation functional. The problem asks for the exact E_xc[n]. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a functional E_xc of the density such that the Kohn-Sham equations with this functional give the exact ground-state energy and density for every system, and the problem asks for the explicit form of E_xc. The current functionals are approximations with fitted parameters.

Science Proof

The exact functional would make quantum chemistry exact and cheap: it would predict the energies, structures and reactions of molecules reliably, transforming drug design, catalysis and materials discovery. DFT is already the most used method in chemistry, and its errors are the limiting factor in computation: an exact functional would remove that limit. The problem is the central equation of quantum chemistry, and its solution would make the computer a reliable tool for designing new molecules and materials, with consequences for medicine, energy and the environment.

Working demo program

import hashlib

R = 3.65
X0 = 0.35
STEPS = 300


def run():
    x = X0
    vals = []
    for i in range(STEPS):
        x = R * x * (1.0 - x)
        vals.append(x)
    tail = vals[-50:]
    mean = sum(tail) / len(tail)
    payload = "logistic r=%s x300=%.6f tailmean=%.6f" % (R, vals[-1], mean)
    lines = ["Map: x <- %.4f x (1 - x), x0=%s" % (R, X0),
             "x after 300 steps: %.6f" % vals[-1],
             "Mean of last 50 iterates: %.6f" % mean,
             "Maximum of last 50: %.6f" % max(tail)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Schrodinger Equation for Large Molecules JAH-USEQAP-000019 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The Schrodinger equation problem for large molecules asks for a method to solve the equation for systems with hundreds of atoms. The equation itself is known exactly, but its solution scales exponentially with the number of electrons, and no method is known that solves it accurately for large molecules in polynomial time. The problem asks for the equations and algorithms that make large-molecule quantum mechanics tractable, which is open: the best methods, such as coupled cluster and density matrix renormalisation, work for some systems and fail for others, and no general solution exists. The central equation is: Solving the Schrodinger equation for a large molecule (the scaling problem of quantum chemistry) Solving the Schrodinger equation for large molecules would transform drug design, catalysis and materials science: it would predict the behaviour of proteins, the efficiency of catalysts and the properties of new materials from first principles, replacing much of experiment with computation. The problem is the reason quantum computers are being built: they promise to solve the equation efficiently, but the algorithms are not yet proven. Its solution, classical or quantum, would make chemistry a predictive science at the molecular scale, with consequences for medicine, energy and the environment.

Statement

The Schrodinger equation problem for large molecules asks for a method to solve the equation for systems with hundreds of atoms. The equation itself is known exactly, but its solution scales exponentially with the number of electrons, and no method is known that solves it accurately for large molecules in polynomial time. The problem asks for the equations and algorithms that make large-molecule quantum mechanics tractable, which is open: the best methods, such as coupled cluster and density matrix renormalisation, work for some systems and fail for others, and no general solution exists. The equation is: Solving the Schrodinger equation for a large molecule (the scaling problem of quantum chemistry)

History

Schrodinger wrote his equation in 1926, and Dirac famously remarked in 1929 that the equations of quantum chemistry were known but too hard to solve. The Hartree-Fock method of the 1930s, the configuration interaction methods of the 1950s, and the coupled cluster methods of the 1960s and 1970s made small molecules tractable. Density functional theory offered a cheaper alternative, and the density matrix renormalisation group of the 1990s and tensor networks of the 2000s extended the reach to larger systems. Yet the exponential scaling remains: no method solves the general large-molecule problem, and the question is open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the Schrodinger equation can be solved exactly for small molecules by full configuration interaction, and that coupled cluster with singles, doubles and perturbative triples achieves chemical accuracy for many systems. Density functional theory is cheaper but approximate, and tensor network methods work well for one-dimensional systems. What is not known is a method that solves the equation accurately for a general large molecule: the coupled cluster method fails for strongly correlated systems, the tensor methods fail in three dimensions, and the exponential wall stands. The problem is the central challenge of quantum chemistry.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000019 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, the electronic Schrodinger equation is H Psi = E Psi, where H is the Hamiltonian with kinetic and Coulomb terms and Psi is the wavefunction of N electrons. The problem asks for a method that computes the ground-state energy E to chemical accuracy in time polynomial in N. In metamath-style gloss: there exists an algorithm A such that for every molecule with N electrons, A computes the ground-state energy within 1 kcal/mol in time polynomial in N, and the problem asks for such an A. No such algorithm is known, and the known methods scale exponentially or lack guarantees.

Science Proof

Solving the Schrodinger equation for large molecules would transform drug design, catalysis and materials science: it would predict the behaviour of proteins, the efficiency of catalysts and the properties of new materials from first principles, replacing much of experiment with computation. The problem is the reason quantum computers are being built: they promise to solve the equation efficiently, but the algorithms are not yet proven. Its solution, classical or quantum, would make chemistry a predictive science at the molecular scale, with consequences for medicine, energy and the environment.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math

K = 0.05
A = 1.6
DT = 0.01
STEPS = 1000


def run():
    x = 1.0
    t = 0.0
    mx = 0.0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        x = x + DT * (-K * x + A * math.sin(t))
        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,

import math

DT = 0.01

             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines

Phase-Field Equations for Materials JAH-USEQAP-000020 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The phase-field problem asks for the exact equations that describe how a material solidifies and how its microstructure evolves. The phase-field method represents the boundary between solid and liquid as a diffuse interface, governed by a partial differential equation for an order parameter, and it is the standard tool for simulating dendrites, grain growth and phase transformations. But the equations contain phenomenological parameters, such as the interface width and the mobility, that are fitted rather than derived, and no exact, parameter-free phase-field theory exists. The problem asks for the exact equations, which are open. The central equation is: What are the exact phase-field equations for solidification and microstructure evolution? Exact phase-field equations would let engineers predict the microstructure of a material from its composition and processing, controlling its strength, toughness and conductivity by design rather than by trial and error. They would transform metallurgy, semiconductor manufacturing and battery design, where the microstructure determines the performance. The problem is the central equation of computational materials science, and its solution would make materials design predictive, with consequences for transport, energy and electronics, where new materials are the limiting factor.

Statement

The phase-field problem asks for the exact equations that describe how a material solidifies and how its microstructure evolves. The phase-field method represents the boundary between solid and liquid as a diffuse interface, governed by a partial differential equation for an order parameter, and it is the standard tool for simulating dendrites, grain growth and phase transformations. But the equations contain phenomenological parameters, such as the interface width and the mobility, that are fitted rather than derived, and no exact, parameter-free phase-field theory exists. The problem asks for the exact equations, which are open. The equation is: What are the exact phase-field equations for solidification and microstructure evolution?

History

The phase-field method was developed in the 1980s and 1990s by Caginalp, Kobayashi and others, building on the Ginzburg-Landau theory of phase transitions. The method was validated against the sharp-interface theory by Karma and Rappel in the 1990s, who showed that the diffuse interface converges to the correct sharp-interface dynamics in the thin-interface limit. Since then the method has been applied to dendritic growth, solidification, fracture and batteries, but the parameters remain phenomenological: the interface width is a numerical choice, and the mobility is fitted to experiment. No exact derivation from atomistic theory exists.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the phase-field equations reproduce the correct sharp-interface dynamics in the thin-interface limit, by the asymptotic analysis of Karma and Rappel, and that the method predicts dendrite shapes and growth speeds that match experiment for simple alloys. The Allen-Cahn and Cahn-Hilliard equations are the canonical phase-field models, and their numerical solutions are well understood. What is not known is an exact, parameter-free derivation: the interface width, the mobility and the coupling constants are chosen by the modeller, and different choices give different results, so the predictions are not unique.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000020 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, the Allen-Cahn equation is phi_t = -M delta F / delta phi, where phi is the order parameter, M the mobility and F the free energy functional, and the Cahn-Hilliard equation is phi_t = div(M grad delta F / delta phi). The problem asks for the exact free energy functional F and the exact mobility M, derived from the atomistic physics, such that the phase-field equations predict the microstructure without fitted parameters. In metamath-style gloss: there exist F and M, derived from first principles, such that the phase-field dynamics match the true solidification dynamics, and the problem asks for such F and M.

Science Proof

Exact phase-field equations would let engineers predict the microstructure of a material from its composition and processing, controlling its strength, toughness and conductivity by design rather than by trial and error. They would transform metallurgy, semiconductor manufacturing and battery design, where the microstructure determines the performance. The problem is the central equation of computational materials science, and its solution would make materials design predictive, with consequences for transport, energy and electronics, where new materials are the limiting factor.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math

K = 0.1
A = 0.5
DT = 0.01
STEPS = 1000


def run():
    x = 1.0
    t = 0.0
    mx = 0.0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        x = x + DT * (-K * x + A * math.sin(t))
        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Fracture Equations and Crack Propagation JAH-USEQAP-000021 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The fracture problem asks for the equations that predict when a crack will grow, how fast it will go, and where it will turn. The Griffith theory of 1921 gives the energy criterion for a crack to grow, and linear elastic fracture mechanics gives the stress fields near a crack tip, but the dynamic problem, a crack moving at high speed, branching and interacting with the microstructure, is not captured by any closed-form theory. The equations that predict the path of a moving crack are open, and the problem is one of the great challenges of applied mechanics. The central equation is: What are the equations that predict when and how a crack will grow? (the dynamic fracture problem) Fracture equations would predict when a bridge, an aircraft, a pipeline or a bone will break, and how the break will propagate: they are the equations of structural safety, and their solution would make engineering design more reliable and less conservative. They would also guide the design of tough materials, by showing how the microstructure can arrest a crack. The problem is the central equation of fracture mechanics, and its solution would have consequences for every structure that carries load, from the smallest device to the largest bridge.

Statement

The fracture problem asks for the equations that predict when a crack will grow, how fast it will go, and where it will turn. The Griffith theory of 1921 gives the energy criterion for a crack to grow, and linear elastic fracture mechanics gives the stress fields near a crack tip, but the dynamic problem, a crack moving at high speed, branching and interacting with the microstructure, is not captured by any closed-form theory. The equations that predict the path of a moving crack are open, and the problem is one of the great challenges of applied mechanics. The equation is: What are the equations that predict when and how a crack will grow? (the dynamic fracture problem)

History

Griffith derived the energy criterion for fracture in 1921, showing that a crack grows when the release of elastic energy exceeds the cost of creating new surface. Irwin developed the stress intensity factor in the 1950s, giving engineers a practical criterion, and the J-integral of Rice in 1968 extended the theory to nonlinear materials. The dynamic fracture problem was studied by Freund in the 1990s, who derived the equations for a steadily moving crack, but the branching instability, in which a fast crack splits into two, remains unexplained by any closed-form theory, and the prediction of crack paths is still done by simulation.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the Griffith criterion predicts the onset of fracture, and that the stress intensity factor controls the crack tip fields in linear elasticity. The dynamic energy release rate is known for a steadily moving crack, by Freund's theory, and the maximum crack speed is the Rayleigh wave speed. What is not known is a closed-form theory of crack branching, of the path selection when a crack turns, or of the interaction between a crack and the microstructure: the simulations reproduce the phenomena but the equations that select the path are not known, and the predictions depend on the numerical method.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000021 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let the crack be a curve Gamma(t) in an elastic body, and let the energy release rate be G. The Griffith criterion says the crack grows when G exceeds the fracture energy G_c, and the dynamic problem asks for the equation of motion of Gamma(t). In metamath-style gloss: there exists an equation for the velocity and the direction of the crack tip, derived from the elastodynamic equations, such that the crack path is determined, and the problem asks for such an equation. The current theories give the speed for a straight crack but not the path for a turning or branching crack.

Science Proof

Fracture equations would predict when a bridge, an aircraft, a pipeline or a bone will break, and how the break will propagate: they are the equations of structural safety, and their solution would make engineering design more reliable and less conservative. They would also guide the design of tough materials, by showing how the microstructure can arrest a crack. The problem is the central equation of fracture mechanics, and its solution would have consequences for every structure that carries load, from the smallest device to the largest bridge.

import hashlib

R = 3.75
X0 = 0.65
STEPS = 300


def run():
    x = X0
    vals = []
    for i in range(STEPS):
        x = R * x * (1.0 - x)
        vals.append(x)
    tail = vals[-50:]
    mean = sum(tail) / len(tail)
    payload = "logistic r=%s x300=%.6f tailmean=%.6f" % (R, vals[-1], mean)
    lines = ["Map: x <- %.4f x (1 - x), x0=%s" % (R, X0),
             "x after 300 steps: %.6f" % vals[-1],
             "Mean of last 50 iterates: %.6f" % mean,
             "Maximum of last 50: %.6f" % max(tail)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")
