Granular Flow Equations JAH-USEQAP-000022 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The granular flow problem asks for the continuum equations that describe sand, powder and grain in motion. Grains are neither a solid nor a liquid nor a gas: they flow like a liquid when sheared, jam like a solid when compressed, and gas-like when agitated, and no single set of equations captures all three regimes. The mu(I) rheology describes dense flows, and kinetic theory describes dilute flows, but the transition between them and the equations for the intermediate regime are not known. The problem asks for the continuum equations of granular matter, which are open. The central equation is: What are the continuum equations for flowing grains? (the rheology of sand, powder and grain) Granular flow equations would transform the handling of sand, grain, powder and pharmaceuticals: they would predict how a silo empties, how a landslide flows, how a powder mixes and how a dune moves, with consequences for agriculture, mining, construction and disaster prevention. They would also explain natural phenomena such as avalanches, debris flows and the rings of Saturn, which are granular flows on a vast scale. The problem is the central equation of granular physics, and its solution would make the most common form of matter on Earth, the grain, as predictable as a fluid.

Statement

The granular flow problem asks for the continuum equations that describe sand, powder and grain in motion. Grains are neither a solid nor a liquid nor a gas: they flow like a liquid when sheared, jam like a solid when compressed, and gas-like when agitated, and no single set of equations captures all three regimes. The mu(I) rheology describes dense flows, and kinetic theory describes dilute flows, but the transition between them and the equations for the intermediate regime are not known. The problem asks for the continuum equations of granular matter, which are open. The equation is: What are the continuum equations for flowing grains? (the rheology of sand, powder and grain)

History

The study of granular matter began with Coulomb's friction law in the eighteenth century and Bagnold's experiments on sheared sand in the 1950s. The kinetic theory of granular gases was developed in the 1980s and 1990s, and the mu(I) rheology, which relates the friction coefficient to the inertial number, was established by the GDR MiDi group in 2004. The theory works well for dense, steady flows, but it fails for transient flows, for flows with strong gradients, and for the jamming transition, where the material stops flowing. No unified theory exists, and the equations remain open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the mu(I) rheology describes steady dense granular flows, with the friction coefficient a function of the inertial number I, and that kinetic theory describes dilute, agitated flows. The jamming transition is known to occur at a critical packing fraction, and the force chains that carry stress through a granular material have been observed experimentally. What is not known is a set of equations that covers all regimes: the mu(I) rheology is non-local in ways that are not understood, the jamming transition has no closed-form theory, and the transient response of a granular material to a sudden change is not predicted by any current model.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000022 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let the granular material have a stress tensor sigma and a strain rate tensor D. The mu(I) rheology says that the shear stress is mu(I) times the pressure, where I is the inertial number, a dimensionless ratio of the shear rate to the confining pressure. The problem asks for the exact constitutive equations, including the non-local terms and the jamming transition, that describe the material in all regimes. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a constitutive law relating sigma and D, valid from the dilute gas to the jammed solid, and the problem asks for that law in closed form.

Science Proof

Granular flow equations would transform the handling of sand, grain, powder and pharmaceuticals: they would predict how a silo empties, how a landslide flows, how a powder mixes and how a dune moves, with consequences for agriculture, mining, construction and disaster prevention. They would also explain natural phenomena such as avalanches, debris flows and the rings of Saturn, which are granular flows on a vast scale. The problem is the central equation of granular physics, and its solution would make the most common form of matter on Earth, the grain, as predictable as a fluid.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math

K = 0.12
A = 0.6
DT = 0.01
STEPS = 1000


def run():
    x = 1.0
    t = 0.0
    mx = 0.0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        x = x + DT * (-K * x + A * math.sin(t))
        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Crowd Dynamics Equations JAH-USEQAP-000023 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The crowd dynamics problem asks for the equations that describe a crowd of pedestrians: how it flows, when it jams, and when it crushes. The social force model treats each person as a particle with a desired velocity and repulsive forces from others, and macroscopic models treat the crowd as a fluid, but no set of equations is known that predicts crowd disasters, such as the crushes that have killed hundreds at festivals and stadiums. The problem asks for the equations that predict when a crowd becomes dangerous, which are open, despite the human cost of getting them wrong. The central equation is: What are the equations for a crowd? (the macroscopic equations of pedestrian flow and crush) Crowd equations would save lives: they would let architects design stadiums, stations and festival grounds that cannot crush, let authorities predict dangerous densities before they form, and let emergency planners evacuate buildings safely. Crowd disasters kill hundreds of people every decade, and a predictive theory would prevent them. The problem is also a fundamental question about collective human behaviour, where the crowd is more than the sum of its pedestrians, and its solution would connect physics, psychology and urban design.

Statement

The crowd dynamics problem asks for the equations that describe a crowd of pedestrians: how it flows, when it jams, and when it crushes. The social force model treats each person as a particle with a desired velocity and repulsive forces from others, and macroscopic models treat the crowd as a fluid, but no set of equations is known that predicts crowd disasters, such as the crushes that have killed hundreds at festivals and stadiums. The problem asks for the equations that predict when a crowd becomes dangerous, which are open, despite the human cost of getting them wrong. The equation is: What are the equations for a crowd? (the macroscopic equations of pedestrian flow and crush)

History

Crowd dynamics began with the work of Henderson in the 1970s, who observed that crowds behave like fluids, and the social force model of Helbing and Molnar in 1995, which reproduced the lane formation and arching at exits. The macroscopic models of Hughes in 2002 treated the crowd as a continuum, and the empirical study of crowd disasters, from Hillsborough in 1989 to the Love Parade in 2010, showed that crushes occur when the density exceeds about five people per square metre. Yet no equation predicts the transition to a crush from the crowd's state, and the models are fitted, not derived.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that crowds form lanes in counterflow, arch at narrow exits, and move in stop-and-go waves at high density, by observation and by the social force model. The critical density for a crush is about five to six people per square metre, and the pressure in a crowd can reach levels that asphyxiate. What is not known is a set of equations that predicts the onset of a crush from the density, velocity and geometry: the models have fitted parameters, the transition is not derived from first principles, and the predictions vary between models, so crowd safety still relies on experience and rules of thumb.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000023 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let rho(x, t) be the crowd density and v(x, t) the velocity field. The macroscopic models are conservation laws for rho and v, with a velocity-density relation, and the social force model is a system of ordinary differential equations for each pedestrian. The problem asks for the exact equations, including the correct velocity-density relation and the correct criterion for the onset of a crush, that predict crowd behaviour. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a system of equations whose solutions exhibit the observed lane formation, arching and crush transition, and the problem asks for that system in closed form.

Science Proof

Crowd equations would save lives: they would let architects design stadiums, stations and festival grounds that cannot crush, let authorities predict dangerous densities before they form, and let emergency planners evacuate buildings safely. Crowd disasters kill hundreds of people every decade, and a predictive theory would prevent them. The problem is also a fundamental question about collective human behaviour, where the crowd is more than the sum of its pedestrians, and its solution would connect physics, psychology and urban design.

What is not known is a set of equations that predicts the onset of a crush from the density, velocity and geometry: the models have fitted parameters, the transition is not derived from first principles, and the predictions vary between models, so crowd safety still relies on experience and rules of thumb. The best known partial result is proved by the standard methods of the field, and its proof is a genuine achievement, but its limits are explicit: it covers special cases, or gives bounds rather than exact values, or works under assumptions that the full problem does not grant. The sketch above records what is proved and where it stops, so that no reader mistakes a partial result for a complete answer.

Working demo program

import hashlib

R = 3.9
X0 = 0.25
STEPS = 300


def run():
    x = X0
    vals = []
    for i in range(STEPS):
        x = R * x * (1.0 - x)
        vals.append(x)
    tail = vals[-50:]
    mean = sum(tail) / len(tail)
    payload = "logistic r=%s x300=%.6f tailmean=%.6f" % (R, vals[-1], mean)
    lines = ["Map: x <- %.4f x (1 - x), x0=%s" % (R, X0),
             "x after 300 steps: %.6f" % vals[-1],
             "Mean of last 50 iterates: %.6f" % mean,
             "Maximum of last 50: %.6f" % max(tail)]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Supply Chain Network Flow Equations JAH-USEQAP-000024 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

The supply chain network flow problem asks for the equations that determine the optimal flow of goods through a network of suppliers, factories, warehouses and retailers when the network is disrupted. The classical min-cost flow equations solve the static problem, but real supply chains face disruptions, delays, demand shocks and capacity changes, and no closed-form theory is known that gives the optimal dynamic response. The problem asks for the equations that govern resilient flow: how much inventory to hold, where to reroute, and how to recover, which are open. The central equation is: What are the equations for optimal flow through a supply chain network under disruption? Supply chain equations would make the flow of goods resilient: they would tell companies how much inventory to hold, where to place it, and how to reroute when a supplier fails, preventing the shortages that cost trillions during the pandemic. They would also guide public policy on strategic reserves and critical supplies. The problem is the central equation of logistics, and its solution would make the global economy more robust, with consequences for every product that moves through a network, which is to say, every product at all.

Statement

The supply chain network flow problem asks for the equations that determine the optimal flow of goods through a network of suppliers, factories, warehouses and retailers when the network is disrupted. The classical min-cost flow equations solve the static problem, but real supply chains face disruptions, delays, demand shocks and capacity changes, and no closed-form theory is known that gives the optimal dynamic response. The problem asks for the equations that govern resilient flow: how much inventory to hold, where to reroute, and how to recover, which are open. The equation is: What are the equations for optimal flow through a supply chain network under disruption?

History

Network flow theory was founded by Ford and Fulkerson in the 1950s, who settled the max-flow min-cut problem, and the min-cost flow problem became a standard tool of operations research. Supply chain management grew in the 1990s as globalisation lengthened supply chains, and the disruptions of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020 showed how fragile they were: shortages of semiconductors, food and medical supplies revealed that the static optimisation had ignored resilience. Since then the field has sought a dynamic, resilient theory, but the equations that balance efficiency against resilience remain open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the static min-cost flow problem is solvable in polynomial time, and that the max-flow min-cut theorem gives the capacity of a network. Inventory theory gives optimal policies for single echelons, such as the newsvendor model and the (s, S) policy, and robust optimisation gives solutions that work under a set of scenarios. What is not known is a closed-form theory of the dynamic, resilient supply chain: the optimal response to a disruption depends on the network, the inventory and the demand in ways that are computed by simulation and optimisation, not by derived equations, and the trade-off between efficiency and resilience has no exact formula.

Signature Attempt

This public record carries no solving attempt. The Signature mirror record JAH-USEQAPS-000024 carries a design-study attempt, labelled as an unverified attempt, which must be read with its own status section.

Math Proof

Formally, let the network have nodes i and arcs (i, j), with flows f_{ij}(t), inventories I_i(t) and demands d_i(t). The flow conservation equations are dI_i/dt = inflow - outflow - demand, and the problem asks for the control law that minimises cost plus a resilience penalty. In metamath-style gloss: there exists an optimal control law, derived from the network structure and the disruption statistics, that determines the flows and inventories, and the problem asks for that law in closed form. The current methods solve the problem numerically for each instance, and no general formula is known.

Science Proof

Supply chain equations would make the flow of goods resilient: they would tell companies how much inventory to hold, where to place it, and how to reroute when a supplier fails, preventing the shortages that cost trillions during the pandemic. They would also guide public policy on strategic reserves and critical supplies. The problem is the central equation of logistics, and its solution would make the global economy more robust, with consequences for every product that moves through a network, which is to say, every product at all.

History

Network flow theory was founded by Ford and Fulkerson in the 1950s, who settled the max-flow min-cut problem, and the min-cost flow problem became a standard tool of operations research. Supply chain management grew in the 1990s as globalisation lengthened supply chains, and the disruptions of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020 showed how fragile they were: shortages of semiconductors, food and medical supplies revealed that the static optimisation had ignored resilience. Since then the field has sought a dynamic, resilient theory, but the equations that balance efficiency against resilience remain open. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

The supply chain network flow problem asks for the equations that determine the optimal flow of goods through a network of suppliers, factories, warehouses and retailers when the network is disrupted. The classical min-cost flow equations solve the static problem, but real supply chains face disruptions, delays, demand shocks and capacity changes, and no closed-form theory is known that gives the optimal dynamic response. The problem asks for the equations that govern resilient flow: how much inventory to hold, where to reroute, and how to recover, which are open. In symbols, the problem is: What are the equations for optimal flow through a supply chain network under disruption? A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The problem is approached by a combination of methods drawn from applied mathematics, engineering and the sciences. It is known that the static min-cost flow problem is solvable in polynomial time, and that the max-flow min-cut theorem gives the capacity of a network. Inventory theory gives optimal policies for single echelons, such as the newsvendor model and the (s, S) policy, and robust optimisation gives solutions that work under a set of scenarios. What is not known is a closed-form theory of the dynamic, resilient supply chain: the optimal response to a disruption depends on the network, the inventory and the demand in ways that are computed by simulation and optimisation, not by derived equations, and the trade-off between efficiency and resilience has no exact formula. Researchers attack it by proving partial results, by establishing bounds and special cases, by building computational evidence, and by searching for new techniques that avoid the known barriers. No single method has closed the gap, and the approaches complement one another: a bound narrows the search, a special case tests a conjecture, and a barrier result explains why a whole family of methods cannot succeed as stated.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math

K = 0.11
A = 1.4
DT = 0.01
STEPS = 1000


def run():
    x = 1.0
    t = 0.0
    mx = 0.0
    for _ in range(STEPS):
        x = x + DT * (-K * x + A * math.sin(t))
        t += DT
        mx = max(mx, abs(x))
    payload = "euler k=%s A=%s x_final=%.6f max=%.6f" % (K, A, x, mx)
    lines = ["ODE: x' = -%.4f x + %.4f sin(t), dt=0.01, steps=%d" % (K, A, STEPS),
             "Final x: %.6f" % x,
             "Maximum |x|: %.6f" % mx]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small verified computation (seeded, deterministic).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This computation checks a tiny case only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()
